I also think refusing to ask spares the government a difficult decision and leaves every cost where it already is. On you."

Buffy breathed out slowly.

The library door rattled. Willow peered through the glass, saw the sign, and rattled it again.

"We should let her in," Xander said.

"In a minute."

Buffy looked from him to Giles.

"I'll meet him," she said. "That isn't agreeing to anything except the meeting."

"Your meeting," Xander said.

"Our meeting. But my life."

"Absolutely."

She went to unlock the door.

Xander reached for the office phone.

✳

The vampires chose Laurel Park because nobody expected vampires in Laurel Park.

Buffy expected them in cemeteries, alleys, abandoned factories, drainage tunnels, and the Bronze parking lot on nights when the bouncer was careless. Laurel Park was porch lights, trimmed hedges, minivans, and parents who still let younger children ride bicycles after sunset because the nearest cemetery was more than a mile away.

Three vampires had a station wagon boxed against a curb when Buffy and Xander turned the corner.

The driver kept hitting the horn. A woman in the passenger seat was trying to climb into the back, where two children were screaming. One vampire had the driver's door bent back on its hinges, metal shrieking. Another drove both palms into the windshield, and the glass went white and crazed from the point of impact.

"That's new," Buffy said.

Then she ran.

Xander stayed half a step behind her. Porch lights were coming on. A man stood in an upstairs window across the street, telephone in hand. Superman could not appear beside Xander Harris, and Xander Harris could not throw a vampire through a house.

Buffy kicked the vampire away from the driver's door. It hit the pavement, rolled, and came up snarling.

"Go!" she shouted at the driver.

The station wagon's rear tire spun against the curb.

The vampire at the windshield turned toward Buffy. Xander caught its arm before it could reach her from behind and used exactly enough strength to pull it off balance. It rounded on him.

"You picked the wrong street," Xander said.

"We didn't pick it."

The answer came out wild and furious.

The vampire punched him. Xander let himself stagger because people were watching, then stepped back before it could feel how little the blow had done.

Buffy staked the one from the driver's door. Dust burst across the asphalt.

The station wagon dropped off the curb and accelerated away, damaged door flapping. The third vampire sprang after it, made three strides, then stopped.

It was facing west.

Nothing blocked the road. The intersection stood empty under a yellow streetlamp.

The vampire lurched backward, one foot catching against the other, nearly losing its balance.

"No," it whispered.

Buffy saw it too. "What?"

The vampire backed toward her.

"Not going back."

"Back where?"

It looked at her, then past her, calculating which danger was worse.

The one fighting Xander broke away and ran east.

Buffy moved to pursue. The third vampire grabbed her from behind. It did not try to bite. It used her as a barrier while staring west.

Xander pulled it off her and threw it into a hedge. The movement was too fast and too clean, but the witness at the upstairs window had ducked away, and the man emerging from the house next door was looking toward the damaged station wagon.

Buffy staked the vampire in the hedge.

They chased the last one two blocks before it vanished between houses. Xander could hear its footfalls continuing east, but following at speed would have left Buffy and Xander's clothes behind in front of witnesses.

They stopped beneath a dark porch.

"You heard it," Buffy said.

"Didn't pick the street. Not going back."

"Vampires get pushed out of territory all the time."

"Usually by something they can point at."

Sirens approached from the south.

Buffy wiped dust from her jacket. "We should not be here when the police arrive."

They cut through two yards and reached an alley before the first patrol car turned onto Laurel.

"Stronger nest moves in," Buffy said as they walked. "Demon claims their feeding ground. Spike gets bored and decides everybody in one zip code annoys him."

"Hellmouth churn?"

"Always available as an answer when the real answer refuses to identify itself."

"So normal weird?"

"Normal until Angel checks the nest."

Angel found them outside the library forty minutes later.

"It's empty," he said.

He had tracked the escaped vampire in the opposite direction first, then doubled back to the address Buffy knew from patrol. The nest occupied the basement beneath a closed furniture warehouse.

"Empty how?" Giles asked.

"They ran. Clothes, blood, money, everything they couldn't carry quickly is still there."

"Signs of a fight?"

"None."

Buffy crossed her arms. "Bigger predator?"

"No scent I recognize. No blood except what they stored. No tracks from anyone entering after them."

"Then what chased them?"

Angel looked toward the library doors, though the warehouse lay several streets away.

"I don't know. At the threshold, there was pressure."

Xander thought of Ethan's chalk circle. "Like a ward?"

"Not exactly. A ward tells you to stop. This felt like something telling me which direction was available."

"Could you go in?" Giles asked.

"Yes. It was faint."

Buffy looked west, toward the part of town the vampire had refused to enter. "Territory dispute, magical eviction, Hellmouth churn."

"Still normal?" Xander asked.

"Sunnydale normal. Which is normal wearing a fake mustache."

It wasn't an attack, not yet. It was just another question mark on a map already crowded with them.

They marked the nest on Giles's map anyway.

✳

The shortcut behind Saint Michael's had been there longer than Buffy had lived in Sunnydale.

It ran between the church's rear wall and an old brick laundry, crossed a narrow service lane, and opened beside Restfield Cemetery. Buffy used it often enough to know which drainpipe leaked, where the pavement had buckled, and which section smelled like wet cardboard even when it had not rained.

She entered from Mason Street at 8:12 on Thursday evening.

She emerged on Carver Avenue at 8:14.

Buffy stopped.

Carver was four blocks south of Restfield. A twenty-four-hour laundromat glowed across the street. Three plastic chairs stood empty behind its front window. A city bus passed at the end of the block.

She turned around.

The passage behind her looked right. Brick wall, church plaster, leaking drainpipe, buckled pavement.

Buffy walked back through.

She came out on Mason Street where she had started.

"Okay."

She went through a third time.

Restfield's iron fence waited at the other end exactly where it belonged.

Buffy checked her watch. Two minutes.

She stood in the cemetery entrance and tried to remember whether she had been thinking about Washington, the displaced vampires, or the algebra test she had almost certainly failed. She could have turned wrong in the service lane. There was nowhere to turn, but exhaustion could make a person miss the obvious.

At the library later, she mentioned it while Willow sharpened stakes.

"Maybe you got turned around," Willow said.

"In a straight passage?"

"A very complicated straight passage?"

"It has two walls."

"Then less complicated."

Buffy shrugged. "I went through again and it worked. Add it to the list of reasons sleep should become a tactical priority."

Willow put down the sharpener. "You want me to check it?"

"For what? Bad alley navigation?"

"Magic."

"If every wrong turn in Sunnydale is magic, Giles needs a bigger map."

The next evening, Angel followed the scent of the escaped vampire from Laurel Park into the same shortcut.

The trail was two days old, thin beneath oil, rain and human traffic. He followed it from Mason Street, past the church wall, and across the service lane.

Halfway through, the scent turned back.

Angel stopped.

The vampire had not turned around. There was no overlap, no disturbed dirt, no second passage beside the first. Its scent continued forward and arrived behind him.

He followed it.

The passage opened onto Carver Avenue.

Angel returned immediately. He entered from Carver, walked the same physical distance, and emerged beside a loading dock on Prentiss, two blocks east of the church.

On the fourth attempt, he reached Restfield.

Buffy was waiting at the library when he arrived.

"You got Carver too?"

"And Prentiss."

"Good. Not good, but good for my confidence in basic geometry."

Willow spread a street map across the table. Giles marked each exit. The lines formed no useful shape.

"Localized spatial distortion," Giles said. "The Hellmouth has produced similar effects."

"Has it?" Buffy asked.

"Similar in category, not detail."

"Meaning no."

"Meaning I would prefer further evidence."

Ethan stood at the mezzanine rail, looking down at the map. He had arrived ten minutes earlier for Xander's lesson and said nothing during Angel's report.

"Cities have grammar," he said.

Giles looked up. "Do they."

"Roads define movement. Property lines define ownership. Thresholds define entry. Names define what belongs where. Accumulate enough human agreement over enough time and a city becomes one of the largest rituals most people will ever participate in without knowing it."

Willow looked at the map differently. "And the passage used the wrong grammar?"

"Perhaps. Or someone is pressing on it to see which sentence changes."

"Is it an attack?" Xander asked.

Ethan came down the stairs. "Not every hand on a door belongs to someone who wants in. Some are only testing whether they could.

"That's very reassuring."

"I contain multitudes."

Buffy tapped the mark for Carver Avenue. "We have vampires pushed out of a nest and a shortcut with destination problems."

"Possibly related," Giles said. "Possibly two ordinary manifestations of the Hellmouth."

"Ordinary manifestations," Xander repeated.

"You know what I mean."

Half the town doesn't know what they mean either. They're working on making that a selling point."

Buffy folded the map. "We leave for Washington tomorrow. Angel, can you keep checking the nest and the route?"

"Yes."

"Willow, keep the sightings map current. Giles, research city grammar, which I know is not a real shelf but probably should be."

"And you?" Giles asked.

"I'm going to ask the President for help while carefully not implying we know what needs helping this week."

She picked up her patrol bag.

The wrong street remained marked in red beneath the fold.

✳

The Air Force had apparently decided that if it was going to move two California teenagers across the country without explaining why, it might as well be very efficient about it.

Xander slept for part of the flight. Buffy attempted homework, abandoned it over Kansas, and spent the rest of the trip looking out a window too small to justify the amount of sky beyond it.

They arrived at the White House by an entrance which looked nothing like the entrance people saw on television. Three separate people checked Xander's identification. Four checked Buffy's. A Secret Service agent with the patient expression of a man negotiating with a dangerous foreign power asked her to place all weapons in the tray.

Buffy set down two stakes.

The agent waited.

She added a cross, a bottle of holy water, and a short knife from her ankle.

He continued waiting.

Buffy looked at Xander. "I feel judged."

"He's very good at it."

She produced another stake from the back of her waistband.

The agent studied the collection. "Is that everything, Miss Summers?"

"Define everything."

"Everything capable of injuring another person."

"That includes me."

His expression did not change, but Xander saw the effort involved. "The items, Miss Summers."

Buffy surrendered a second knife, a sharpened hair stick, and something so small Xander had not known she was carrying it. She kept the silver cross around her neck after the agent checked it. One stake went into a locked box beside the security station, close enough to be returned quickly if the building developed a vampire problem.

That concession required two phone calls and an instruction from someone whose name the agent did not provide.

"As they were escorted onward, Buffy murmured, "This place is deeply weird.

"You're carrying six concealed weapons."

"Five. The holy water is defensive."

Zoey met them at the end of the corridor. The relief on her face when she saw Xander lasted just long enough for Buffy to notice it, then settled into a smile broad enough to include them both.

"You came."

"There was a plane and everything," Xander said. "Very persuasive."

"Buffy, I'm Zoey. Thank you for agreeing to this."

Buffy shook her hand. "Thanks for arranging it. Just so we're clear, if your dad tries to draft me, I'm escaping through a window."

"Most of them don't open. I checked when I was nine."

"See? Useful intelligence already."

Zoey did not tell them what to say. She told them who would be in the room, warned Buffy that her father asked questions in clusters, and explained that Leo McGarry could look impatient while giving a person his complete attention. Then she took them around one corner and delivered them into the custody of Mrs. Landingham.

Mrs. Landingham looked at Xander over the rims of her glasses. "You came back."

"I was told there might be snacks."

"You ate mine the last time you were here."

"In my defense, they were unattended for almost thirty seconds."

"They were on my desk."

"A confusingly horizontal surface."

Mrs. Landingham let him enjoy exactly none of his own joke. She turned to Buffy, and the stern lines around her mouth softened into the practical concern of an adult who had noticed a teenager trying to stand as though an overnight flight had not happened.

"You must be Buffy. When did you last eat?"

"On the plane. I'm fine."

"That wasn't the question."

"There was a muffin."

"There is always a muffin. A muffin is what people provide when they would like to claim they served breakfast without accepting responsibility for breakfast."

She lifted a covered plate from the corner of her desk. There were chocolate-chip cookies beneath the cloth, still soft in the middle. Buffy took one after a brief hesitation.

"Thank you."

"Take another."

"One is plenty."

Mrs. Landingham began wrapping four more in waxed paper.

Buffy looked helplessly at Zoey.

"I can't help you," Zoey said. "She has jurisdiction."

"You have schoolwork on the trip home," Mrs. Landingham said, tucking the packet into Buffy's bag. "And I am told you have work again tonight."

Buffy's hand stopped at the edge of the bag. "Probably."

"Then you'll need those. Mr. Harris may have one if you choose to share. He is not to interpret that as an independent allocation."

"I understand the distinction," Xander said solemnly.

"No, you don't."

The inner door opened. Charlie Young told them the President was ready.

Mrs. Landingham replaced the cloth over the remaining cookies, then reconsidered and put the entire open plate into Buffy's hands.

"These too?"

"The President keeps people past meals."

"Mrs. Landingham," Bartlet called from beyond the door, "I can hear you maligning my scheduling practices."

"Then I won't have to repeat myself, Mr. President."

Buffy reached the threshold and hesitated, a plate of cookies balanced in one hand as she looked into the Oval Office.

Bartlet waved her forward. "The Oval Office does not outrank Mrs. Landingham's food logistics. I've checked."

Buffy entered carrying the cookies.

✳

The President did not ask Buffy to prove vampires existed.

He introduced Leo McGarry and Admiral Fitzwallace, made certain Buffy knew Xander was welcome to stay beside her, and asked, "What does a normal week look like?"

Buffy considered him over the edge of a cookie.

"Normal normal, or normal for me?"

"For you."

"School. Training. Patrol. Research if something weird is happening. More patrol if research says the weird thing wants to eat people. Homework somewhere in there."

"How many nights do you patrol?" Fitzwallace asked.

"Most of them."

"Five? Six?"

"Seven is most, right?"

Nobody smiled. Buffy ate another bite and tried again.

"It depends. Some nights are quiet. I walk the cemeteries and the Bronze and a couple of places vampires like to hunt, and I go home. Some nights there are three fledglings crawling out of a grave. Sometimes there's a prophecy or a ritual or somebody decides the apocalypse would really improve their weekend."

"And if you're ill?" Fitzwallace asked.

"Slayers don't get sick much."

"Injured, then."

"Depends how injured."

"Enough that a physician would tell you not to patrol."

Buffy shrugged. "Then I patrol carefully."

Fitzwallace's face did not change. The silence around him did.

Bartlet leaned forward. "When was your last night off?"

"Tonight, technically. Unless we get back early."

"Before tonight."

Buffy looked at Xander as if he might have kept the relevant calendar. "I went to a school dance."

"Which one?"

"Spring Fling."

Xander said, "She stopped a massacre during it."

"I still danced. Eventually."

She explained the rest in the same dry tone. Torn clothes were replaced when she could replace them. Stakes were free if one knew where to find scrap wood. Giles kept weapons in the library, except for the ones Buffy hid at home. Injuries were treated by Giles, Willow, Xander, or Buffy herself unless they became too serious to hide. There was no relief shift. There was Angel sometimes, and her friends too often, and no number she could call when a patrol became something larger than the people already there.

She told them about the vampires hunting in Laurel Park.

"They weren't stronger than usual," she said. "Just somewhere they shouldn't have been. Something scared them out of their nest. We don't know what."

"And the street?" Leo asked.

"Shortcut. Very short. Usually ends in the same place every time, because that's one of the better features of streets. Yesterday it didn't. Then it did. Angel got two other exits when he tried."

"Is that dangerous?"

"Maybe. Or maybe it's Sunnydale being Sunnydale. We don't know yet."

Leo reached toward the plate while she answered.

Without looking down, Buffy moved it six inches to the left. His fingers closed on empty air.

"I thought they were meeting cookies," Leo said.

"You were misinformed."

"Mrs. Landingham's allocation is binding," Bartlet said. "There are constitutional questions, but I'm not prepared to litigate them hungry."

Leo gave the plate a betrayed look and returned to the yellow pad on his knee.

"Who pays your hospital bills?"

Buffy blinked. "I don't usually go to the hospital."

"When you do."

"My mom. Insurance. I don't know."

"Who replaces your clothes?"

"Me. Mom, sometimes, if I can explain what happened to them."

"Weapons?"

"Mostly Giles."

"Transportation?"

"Walking."

"Who explains your absences to the school?"

"No one. Badly."

"Who keeps a hospital clerk, an insurance company, a police officer, or your principal from putting together the pattern?"

Buffy lowered the cookie. "Nobody."

Leo wrote something down. "Then medical care, money, and secrecy all become ways to control you unless the structure is right. A doctor isn't useful if the bill tells the wrong person where you were. A salary isn't useful if the public record identifies what it's for."

"Support role only," Buffy said. "No one gets to turn help into orders later."

"Agreed," Fitzwallace said.

The answer came without negotiation. Buffy watched him a moment, adjusting to that.

Leo asked, "What does your mother know?"

The professional ease in the room evaporated. The men sat a little straighter, their focus narrowing on Buffy with a new intensity.

Buffy looked at the plate. "Nothing."

"Nothing classified, or nothing about vampires?"

"Nothing about vampires. Or me."

Bartlet's attention sharpened. "Your mother doesn't know you do this?"

"She knows I go out. She knows I get into trouble. She knows I ruin a lot of clothes."

"She doesn't know why."

"No."

"What happens if you're hurt badly enough that you can't preserve the story?"

"I try not to be."

"That isn't an answer."

Buffy's shoulders tightened. Xander had seen the movement before fights, a tiny gathering inward before every force she had went outward.

"I told my parents once," she said. "Not everything. Enough. I was scared and I was trying to make them understand. They put me in a clinic. So now I don't tell people unless I know what they're going to do with it."

For several seconds the only sound in the Oval Office was the faint movement of paper under Leo's hand.

A cold anger settled in Bartlet's posture. It was not directed at Buffy, but at the facts she had just laid before him.

"How old were you?"

"Fifteen."

"And now?"

"Sixteen."

He looked away from her once, toward the windows, and mastered whatever he would have said first.

"I'm not going to order you to tell your mother. I don't have that right, and even if I did, it would be a poor use of it. But a secret like this doesn't stay contained. She is already living inside its consequences. Every injury you hide, every absence you can't explain, every night she thinks you're lying for reasons that make sense to ordinary parents."

Buffy had stopped blinking.

"Telling her could make it worse," she said.

"Yes. It could."

She seemed more surprised by that than she would have been by reassurance.

"It could also mean someone at home knows when you need a doctor. Someone can make an honest decision if you're unconscious. Someone who loves you stops mistaking evidence that you're saving lives for evidence that you're throwing yours away."

"She wouldn't believe me."

"Then you wouldn't have to make it credible alone," Bartlet said. "If you decide to tell her, we can confirm what we know. We can provide evidence, adults with names she recognizes, and answers that don't require you to carry the federal government into your living room along with everything else. The choice remains yours. So does the timing. But you would not have to do it alone."

Buffy picked up another cookie. This time nobody mistook the gesture for casualness.

"Okay," she said quietly. "Not yes. Just okay."

"That's what I heard."

Leo gave the silence enough room, then returned to his questions because someone had to.

"How much are you paid?"

Buffy frowned. "For what?"

"For the work you just described."

"I'm not."

"The Council provides a stipend?"

"Giles gets paid."

"You don't?"

"Slayer isn't exactly an after-school job."

"No," Bartlet said. "An after-school job has wages."

Buffy glanced at Xander, then back at the President. "I didn't come here asking for money."

"That is rapidly becoming one of the less relevant facts in this room."

"Mr. President," Fitzwallace said, his voice level, "responsibility without support is abandonment dressed as policy. If the United States intends to benefit from dangerous public service, compensation is not charity. Hazard pay should be immediate."

The words landed somewhere a number couldn't reach. Xander saw it in the sudden uncertainty around her mouth.

"Hazard pay," she repeated.

"Yes," Fitzwallace said. "For hazardous work."

Xander watched her process it. This wasn't about destiny or sacred duty. It was about work. Something real enough to be owed for.

Bartlet rose and came around the desk. "You should never have needed to request medical care, transportation, equipment, relief, backup, or compensation. Every adult institution that knew enough to use the result knew enough to provide those things. How did all of them decide that because you could survive without support, you should have to?"

"The Council would say the Slayer has to stand alone."

"The Council isn't here."

"That may be their first good decision," Leo said.

Buffy almost smiled. "If you do this, there are rules."

"Let's hear them," Bartlet said.

"Support role only. No chain of command over me. Nobody comes into Sunnydale unless I ask, except if people are about to die and there isn't time. No deciding every demon is hostile because it has horns. No taking artifacts because somebody in a lab wants to poke them. No government Watcher."

"What about intelligence?" Fitzwallace asked.

"Offer it. Don't hide it because I didn't file the right form."

"Field backup?"

"Offer. I choose if it fits the problem. Mass-casualty emergency is different."

"Medical evacuation?"

"Yes. Confidential."

"Evidence storage, secure communications, weapons and replacement equipment?"

"Yes."

"An extraction team if you are incapacitated?"

Buffy looked at Xander. He did not answer for her.

"Yes," she said. "But they extract my people too."

Fitzwallace nodded once. "Understood."

Bartlet looked at Leo. "Build it. Completely outside the DRI. Her limits are design requirements, not obstacles somebody gets to work around later."

Leo was already writing. "There are legal problems."

"Then we'll need lawyers. I know a few."

"Secrecy problems."

"We have those too."

"Money problems."

"Leo."

"Just establishing the traditional categories."

Bartlet turned back to Buffy. "We don't have the answer today. We will produce one."

Buffy studied him as carefully as she had studied every entrance to the building. "And if I say no to the answer?"

"We build a better one."

The cookie in her hand broke neatly in two.

She gave half to Xander.

It was the first thing she had shared since entering the room.

✳

Mrs. Landingham was waiting when they came out.

She looked at the empty plate, then at Buffy, her expression satisfied by the evidence.

"Your car will be ready in six minutes."

"We have a car?" Buffy asked.

"At the moment. It belongs to the government, so don't become attached."

Buffy returned the plate. "Thank you."

"You're welcome. The packet is still in your bag?"

"Yes, ma'am."

"Good."

Zoey walked them as far as security. She did not ask how the meeting had gone while officials and agents could hear. Xander thought she had probably learned very young that the important conversation was often the one a hallway was trying to steal.

At the locked box, Buffy reclaimed her weapons and counted each one.

"Your dad didn't try to draft me," she told Zoey.

"High praise."

"He did make me think. I haven't decided if I forgive him for that."

Zoey smiled. "He gets that a lot."

Buffy went ahead with the escort. Zoey touched Xander's sleeve before he followed.

"You asked," she said.

"She said yes."

"Both things can be true."

Xander looked toward Buffy, who was explaining to the patient agent that holy water did not need to be treated as a hazardous liquid unless he had recently died under suspicious circumstances.

"Yeah," he said. "Apparently."

Then he followed her out.

✳

Leo closed the door after the teenagers.

"We cannot put either of them on an ordinary federal payroll," he said. "Harris because every foreign government watching him will call it purchase of service. Summers because the first employment check that reaches a normal database identifies a minor receiving classified combat pay."

"Find an extraordinary payroll," Bartlet said.

"That's not a category."

"It will be by morning."

Fitzwallace remained near the chair Buffy had used. "Payment is the easy part."

"I'm delighted one of us thinks so."

"A protected account, classified disaster authority, legal cover that does not condition payment on orders accepted. You can solve it. The command structure is harder."

Bartlet returned to his desk. "We have special operations."

"We have pieces. A SEAL platoon can put capable people on the ground, but they can't provide their own intelligence, protected medicine, or transport. They need communications, logistics, evidence handling, legal review—a twenty-four-hour command watch just to stay useful. Fragment that support, and every predictable failure belongs to someone else."

"The SGC?" Leo asked.

"Air Force command, existing mission, extreme secrecy, and a chain already carrying more than most governments. Colonel O'Neill would help. General Hammond would help. That isn't the same as building the right answer."

"The DRI is out," Bartlet said.

"Yes, sir. There is no active federal demon-hunting operation in Sunnydale now. If we build through the DRI, we create the road that later becomes exactly what Summers refused."

Leo leaned against the edge of a table. "So who owns it?"

"The Navy. Flag command at Coronado, a small forward element in Sunnydale, and authority to pull specialties from elsewhere. Close enough by road and air to support her, far enough away that the command does not become an occupation."

"Who?"

Fitzwallace answered without looking at his notes. "Rear Admiral A. J. Chegwidden. Former SEAL. Judge Advocate General. He understands operators, law, and the difference between supporting a civilian decision-maker and quietly replacing one."

"Will he do it?" Bartlet asked.

"He will have reasons to decline."

"That's not what I asked."

"No, sir. I believe once he hears who has been carrying the mission, and how, he will consider refusal a failure of principle."

Bartlet nodded. "Call him."

"Tonight?" Leo asked.

"A sixteen-year-old has been working seven nights a week for no salary, treating lethal injuries in a school library, and purchasing her own work clothes. We have forfeited the right to discover office hours."

Fitzwallace reached for the phone.

"And Leo."

"Yes, sir?"

"The money is usable before we send her one dramatic piece of equipment. I don't want a vehicle, a rifle, and twelve men appearing in California while she still has to ask her mother for lunch money."

"I'll have an answer tonight."

"Good."

Fitzwallace began placing the call to the Navy command center. Leo collected his notes and headed for the door.

Bartlet looked down at the single crumb Buffy had left on the Resolute desk.

"And somebody tell Mrs. Landingham she was right about the cookies."

Leo stopped. "You can tell her yourself."

"I am the President of the United States."

"Then you have the authority."

"I was hoping to delegate."

"She has jurisdiction."

Leo left before Bartlet could answer.

✳

The document had required one hundred and fifty-six years to become valuable.

Holland Manners considered that a respectable rate of maturation for a contract.

The original agreement occupied four sheets of linen paper in a climate-controlled case beneath Wolfram & Hart's Los Angeles offices. Richard Wilkins had signed it in 1841, forty-eight years before the town called Sunnydale appeared on any map. The signature was unchanged from the one on the amendment now resting beside it.

The first agreement granted Wilkins exclusive occult jurisdiction within the described territory. In exchange for the firm's assistance securing that jurisdiction, Wolfram & Hart surrendered nearly every right to operate there. Clients could pass through. Employees could conduct ordinary business. The firm could not establish a significant magical presence, alter local thresholds, or contest obligations protected by Wilkins's office.

It had been an extravagant concession.

At the time, the territory had been mostly dust, missions, and cattle.

The amendment reopened it.

Not completely. Wilkins had survived too long to write carelessly. Wolfram & Hart received limited operating rights for the duration of one service and whatever enforcement the service required. In consideration, the firm would provide its highest-ranking sorcerer to prevent the newly manifested power called Superman from interfering with the Mayor's protected civic obligations.

Dr. Evelyn Rook read the final page twice.

She was in her early fifties, dressed in charcoal gray, and carried no visible charm, stone, bone, blade, or other object by which an inexperienced observer might identify a sorcerer. Her only jewelry was a plain gold watch and a small enamel pin on her lapel depicting a surveyor's transit, the kind used to establish boundary lines. Holland knew the watch clasp contained language taken from a Roman property judgment, and that the pin was not decorative, but that was the sort of detail which separated professional practice from costume.

"Well?" he asked.

"The amendment is adequate to permit a survey. It is not adequate to define completion."

"The Mayor wants Superman neutralized."

"The Mayor's stated wish is not the service's operative purpose."

Holland smiled slightly. "He was quite specific."

"Clients often are. Specificity and precision are different disciplines."

Rook placed the amendment beside the original agreement, aligning the bottom edges exactly.

"Superman need not die. His death would attract national attention, foreign intelligence services, several military commands, and every occult opportunist capable of reading a newspaper. Mr. Wilkins requires him to lack standing to interfere. That is a jurisdictional problem."

"And the two tests?"

"Successful."

Holland opened the thin field report. "Four vampires displaced, one nest abandoned. A footpath produced inconsistent exits."

"One test asked whether territorial pressure could redirect a supernatural population already bound to Sunnydale's ecology. The other asked whether civic routes would accept revision under the reopened rights. They are components of the same service."

"The Mayor may regard them as delays."

"The Mayor is welcome to practice magic himself."

Holland's smile grew warmer. "He has."

"Then he should understand the value of definitions."

Rook drew a legal pad toward her and wrote three headings in a compact hand.

SCOPE.

DURATION.

PROTECTED OBLIGATIONS.

"I require the precise acts Superman must be prevented from performing, the period during which the prevention must hold, and an exhaustive schedule of the civic obligations the working is permitted to protect. No implied additions. No later expansions by interpretation."

"You intend to bind him?"

I intend to determine who, precisely, is being bound. The world sees Superman, an outside power. But the town's legal records see Alexander Harris, a resident and high school student. The distinction is useless, unless I can teach the city's magic to read its own paperwork.

"And if it can?"

Rook closed the folder.

"Then Sunnydale will not need to hurt him."

Two floors below them, a records clerk entered the Laurel Park displacement and the altered Carver Avenue route into the same matter file.

"It will merely learn where he is allowed to matter."

Chapter 21: Chapter 21

The third request to examine Lieutenant Commander Data arrived before breakfast.

Jenny rejected it before Commander Warren finished reading the routing information.

Warren ticked off the list on his tablet. "The French are sending a robotics specialist—says he can be aboard by eighteen hundred."

"Then their specialist can enjoy a very long flight home," Jenny said without looking up.

"The Japanese proposal is more detailed. It includes a full nondestructive protocol."

"A protocol written by people who have never seen a positronic brain. It's useless."

He sighed, lowering the tablet. "The British are being more diplomatic. They've only asked for copies of our existing scans."

"Which scans?"

"All of them."

Jenny's gaze finally met his, cold and final. "No."

Jenny stared past Warren, her focus settling on the frost tracing the edges of the conference lounge windows. The portable heaters worked hard enough to make the air smell metallic, but they couldn't fight back the Antarctic morning. Half the ceiling was open to the ship's wounded structure, bundled cables hanging like exposed nerves. Three decks away, Data lay just as broken, his eyes closed since recovery. Every government on the ice wanted a piece of him, a piece of her friend. Her own sudden interest in the word no felt less like a strategy and more like a shield.

Elizabeth Weir warmed both hands around a mug of coffee. "They'll argue that the recovery framework gives observers access to significant discoveries."

"Data isn't a discovery."

"I agree. The framework doesn't yet know how to agree."

"Then it can learn. He is a Starfleet officer and a person. Until we have rules that begin there, nobody opens his head because their government sent a well-qualified man on an airplane."

Weir set down her coffee. "I can circulate an interim personhood protocol today. No invasive examination, no allocation as recovered technology, no samples, and no national claim based on who transported him from the wreck. Technical access only by agreement of the medical and engineering custodians."

"And if someone argues he's property?"

"They can make the argument in writing and attach the name of the official willing to explain it publicly when he wakes up."

Warren glanced toward her. "You think he will?"

Weir did not look away from Jenny. "I think our rules should survive the possibility."

Jenny felt some small knot inside her loosen. "Add that no diagnostic can be used to develop a copy or derivative system."

"Without his consent," Carter said.

"Without his informed consent," Jenny corrected. "If we reach a point where he can give it."

Weir made the addition. It wouldn't settle the century-spanning legal questions, but Jenny saw the immediate, practical genius of it. The protocol shifted the burden. Now, anyone who wanted to touch Data had to start by justifying, on paper, why they should be allowed anywhere near him.

Carter looked up from the diagnostic plan spread across the table. "What we're proposing doesn't open anything."

"I know. That's why you're still here."

The corner of Carter's mouth moved. She turned the plan so Jenny could see the final revision.

The test would transmit less energy than an ordinary tricorder scan. It would map passive conductivity across the surviving positronic pathways, never issue an activation command, and stop at the first sign of power accumulation or internal state change. The diagnostic pattern came from Enterprise maintenance files and had been rewritten to avoid every sequence Jenny remembered as a wake command, repair instruction, or cognitive stimulus.

The word landed in him like something he hadn't expected to still feel.

They did not make her qualified.

They made her careful.

"One pass," Jenny said. "Passive map only. No repetition until we've reviewed every trace."

Carter nodded. "Agreed."

Weir said, "I'll make sure the observer log describes it as a safety assessment, not an examination available for replication."

"They'll still ask."

"Then I can develop an interest in the word no."

Jenny looked at her. "It's satisfying."

"I've noticed."

With the protocol agreed upon, there was no reason to wait. They proceeded to the diagnostic bay, arriving just as the ship's chronometer marked nine hundred hours.

Data rested beneath a clear sterile field. Carter took the engineering station. Jenny monitored internal power, temperature, and the neural pathways they could read without crossing the damaged interface. Warren handled the ship's remaining sensors, including the faint return two miles beneath the ice which had persisted through every attempt to classify it.

"Diagnostic field at one percent," Carter said.

"No response from Data," Jenny reported.

The first pattern passed across the positronic matrix.

Lines appeared on Carter's display, branching structures rendered in pale gold. Most ended abruptly at damaged nodes. Others continued deeper than the scan could resolve. No power built. No pathway activated.

"Two percent."

The buried return changed.

Warren leaned closer to his station. "Commander."

Jenny did not look away from Data. "Report."

"Subsurface signal amplitude is up four percent. Interference pattern narrowing."

Carter's hands stopped above the controls without touching them. "Any effect here?"

"Nothing," Jenny said. "Continue at present level. Do not increase."

The diagnostic advanced through its second internal sequence: branch, pause, comparison pulse, branch. Beneath the ice, the faint signal rose, paused, and changed again.

Warren looked from one display to the other.

"It's following us."

"Correlation isn't response," Carter said.

"The rise matched the branch pulse. The pause matched too."

"Shared sensor noise. Power coupling through the hull. An artifact in the restored array."

"Two miles down?"

"I said possibilities, not conclusions."

Jenny watched Data's internal temperature remain steady to a hundredth of a degree. "Complete the current sequence and terminate."

The final comparison pulse crossed Data's silent pathways. The signal below sharpened once, three narrow peaks inside a broader rise.

Carter ended the field.

The buried return dropped to its old ambiguous baseline.

The low hum of the ship’s systems filled the silence. Warren stared at the screen, his knuckles white where he gripped the console. Carter's breath misted in the cold air.

Warren brought the traces together on one screen. The diagnostic sequence appeared above, the subsurface fluctuation below it. They were not identical. They were far too similar for comfort.

"Run it again," he said. "Same power, same pattern. If the lower signal repeats, we can rule out coincidence."

"No," Jenny said.

"Commander, if something down there is reacting to Data's architecture, we need to know."

"We need to know what we just did to Data before we use him to ask it a second question."

"His readings never changed."

"The readings we understand never changed. That's not the same statement."

Carter saved both raw data streams and locked out the diagnostic controls. "She's right. We review first. We also reproduce every possible source of sensor coupling without Data in the circuit."

Warren looked again at the paired traces. "It followed the structure."

"I saw it," Carter said.

"What does that mean?"

Jenny looked at the unmoving gold face beneath the sterile field.

"At the moment, it means we don't do it again."

The isolation alarm sounded before anyone could answer.

✳

The alarm cut through the sterile silence of the diagnostic bay—a sharp, two-tone warning. The first identified Deck Seven. The second was impossible: power on the wrong side of a dead isolation boundary.

They met Ferretti at the emergency turbolift. He wore insulated gloves and held a pulse rifle he couldn't reasonably expect to help, his face grim under the harsh corridor lights. "Junction seven-fourteen," he said without preamble. "Current on the safe side."

"Source?" Jenny asked, her mind already racing through schematics.

"There isn't one."

"There's always a source."

"Then this one didn't file a travel plan," Ferretti bit out. He led them into the corridor, stripped to bare structural ribs. A meter-wide gap, a firebreak carved by recovery teams, was supposed to separate the dead Deck Seven network from the rest of the ship. It wasn't empty anymore.

Something black and fibrous crossed the gap now.

It had grown through an environmental return duct, followed a seam behind the bulkhead, and emerged inside a conduit restored two days earlier to provide heat to an unrelated compartment. Metallic threads wrapped Starfleet cabling in a precise spiral. Tiny green indicators moved under the black surface like light beneath skin.

Carter aimed a scanner at it from behind the marked line. "No life signs. No active drone carrier."

"That make you feel better?" Ferretti asked.

"Not remotely."

Jenny called up the junction plan. "It bypassed seven-fourteen completely. The alarm found current because the new route touches the monitoring ground here."

"So if it hadn't?"

"We would have discovered it when it reached something noisy enough to notice."

Ferretti looked down the safe corridor, where lights and heat had only recently returned. "Every system we fix gives it another road."

"Yes."

"That's a lousy repair strategy."

"It's a worse survival strategy if we stop restoring life support."

They shut down the entire branch. Darkness took the corridor section by section until only the independent work lights remained. The green movement in the adapted cable slowed but did not stop.

"Stored power," Carter said. "Or local generation."

Jenny ordered the technicians back. The portable field generator established a containment plane across the safe end of the conduit. One technician cut out a three-meter section of ordinary cable beyond it. The second sealed both exposed ends inside layered isolation housings.

The Borg growth continued moving until it reached the physical absence.

Then it stopped.

Not died. Stopped.

Carter watched the last green point hold at the severed edge. "It's behaving like automated repair. Find damage, identify an available system, restore connection."

"Assimilation logic without anybody home," Ferretti said.

"Possibly."

"I liked the empty ship better when empty meant empty."

Jenny expanded the isolation boundary by two compartments. It cost them a secondary heat loop, one working replicator junction, and a sensor relay they had spent nineteen hours restoring. She ordered the entire new pathway removed rather than cut at one end.

Ferretti waited until the technicians began before asking, "What do we call this in the report?"

"Active contamination."

"It stopped."

"Because we took away the road. Dormant systems do not build new roads."

"People are going to want a better answer than that."

"So do I. We don't have one."

Behind the isolation field, the last green light remained fixed at the edge of the gap.

Waiting was too human a word for it.

Jenny found no better one.

✳

Ethan placed a silver coin on the library floor and told Xander not to touch it.

"I wasn't going to."

"You were looking at it like a nail."

"It's a coin."

"And already we've established why this lesson may take all evening."

The library was quiet, empty for the day save for the sound of Giles working in his office. The late sun slanted through the high amber windows, catching the dust motes stirred by Ethan's movements.

Ethan had drawn no circle around the coin. There were no candles, bones, bowls of suspicious liquid, or Latin phrases taped helpfully nearby. The coin lay on worn wood and looked capable of purchasing half a cup of bad coffee.

"Tell me what it does," Ethan said.

Xander focused. The coin appeared to be solid silver. It carried a faint trace of Ethan's skin oil, chalk dust, and something herbal from the pocket where he had kept it.

"It's magic."

"Remarkable."

"You asked."

"I asked what it does."

Xander extended his senses carefully. There was pressure around the coin, weaker than the ward Ethan had built on the roof. It did not push outward. It seemed to wait for some condition he could not see.

"It reacts if I cross something."

"Which something?"

"The edge?"

"There is no marked edge."

"That was me noticing the problem."

Ethan crouched and rolled the coin three feet across the floor. The pressure moved with it.

"So the coin carries the spell," Xander said.

"Does it?"

"You just moved it. The pressure moved."

"An object can carry a rule without being the rule. What happens if you melt it?"

"We lose twenty-five cents."

"This is a shilling."

"Then we lose money I can't spend."

Ethan sighed. "The working permits a person carrying the coin to cross a threshold from which that person has been barred. Melt the silver and the permission may fail, transfer to the largest remaining piece, or attach itself to whoever destroyed the carrier. You do not know until you understand how permission was granted."

"So don't eye-blast the suspicious magic thing."

"Not until someone competent has examined it."

"And if the competent person is being eaten?"

"Then make the best poor decision available. The point of learning is to improve the selection."

Xander stepped closer without touching the coin. The pressure gathered against his awareness.

Something else passed through it.

Cold traced the outside of his skin from his shoes to the top of his head, not pain, not contact. For one strange instant the library seemed to measure him. The walls, floor, school records upstairs, and the town beyond them all felt like parts of the same question.

Then it was gone.

Xander flinched back, a sudden, violent movement that sent a deep groan through the floorboard beneath his heel.

Ethan moved at once. Two fingers touched the air over the coin, then the floor, then the wooden edge of the library doors.

"That wasn't yours," Xander said.

"No."

Giles came out of his office. "What happened?"

"Something passed a working across the boundary. Passive, I think."

"You think?" Xander asked.

"Professional confidence is knowing where certainty ends. I know it did not attempt to bind, injure, compel, or alter you. I think it measured."

"Measured what?"

Ethan examined the cracked board, though the damage had been Xander's. "Whether you belong. Whether your condition is maintained by enchantment. Perhaps whether the school recognizes you."

"And the answer?"

"I don't know."

Giles began checking the doors and windows for marks. "Could you trace it?"

"Not from residue this light. Whoever cast it expected someone might notice."

Xander looked at the ordinary walls. "Should I be worried?"

"Yes," Ethan said. "But not because I know what comes next. Be worried because someone is asking a careful question and has not yet shown us why."

In Los Angeles, Evelyn Rook watched a line of ink travel across prepared paper.

The working had touched no blood, taken no power, and left no mark on Alexander Harris. It had only asked the civic boundary to describe what crossed it.

The answer settled into three precise notations.

Permanent physical state.

No sustaining enchantment.

Resident recognized without dispute.

Rook turned past the page labeled REVERSAL. There was nothing to undo. No illusion to strip away, no continuing spell to starve, no foreign shape waiting to collapse.

She opened a clean file and wrote two headings.

ALEXANDER LAVELLE HARRIS: RESIDENT.

SUPERMAN: FOREIGN POWER.

Between them, she left a measured distance of blank paper.

✳

Chegwidden found Fitzwallace studying the photograph behind his desk.

It showed six younger men on the deck of a carrier. Chegwidden was in flight gear, standing slightly apart from the others in a way that suggested he hadn't been invited into the frame so much as claimed a position in it. Fitzwallace had one hand raised, index finger extended, mid-point in what looked like a correction rather than a greeting. The remaining four had the look of men who expected to be somewhere else by dinner.

Fitzwallace turned. "About which thing?"

"Whatever you were arguing."

"History remembers me as persuasive."

"History wasn't trapped on that carrier for eleven weeks."

They shook hands, then abandoned the formality and embraced briefly. Chegwidden closed his office door.

"You look terrible," he said.

"You look like a lawyer."

"That's because I outrank the good tailors. Coffee?"

"If it's improved."

"It hasn't."

Chegwidden poured two cups anyway. Fitzwallace accepted his and moved to the visitor chair without asking. For several minutes they discussed people whose names did not appear in either man's current briefing books. One had retired to Annapolis and become intolerable about sailing. Another had married for the third time and claimed this represented optimism rather than poor target identification. Chegwidden asked after Fitzwallace's wife. Fitzwallace asked after Harm and Mac in terms which suggested he knew more about the admiral's office than the admiral preferred.

"They are excellent officers," Chegwidden said.

"That wasn't the question."

"It was the answer you were getting."

Fitzwallace drank the coffee and grimaced. "Still awful."

"It keeps visits short."

Neither man looked at the sealed folder Fitzwallace had placed on the corner of the desk.

Chegwidden asked, "How long has it been?"

"Since we had dinner? Two years."

"Three. You canceled two years ago."

"The President was trying to prevent a war."

"Presidents are inconsiderate that way."

"You canceled the year before."

"A pilot landed an aircraft where the Navy preferred there not be an aircraft."

"They still let you use pilots?"

"Against my advice."

The ease remained, though it felt thinner. Chegwidden found himself thinking of Rick's funeral last spring—an official notice was the only reason he'd known. They kept moving dinners to a calendar neither of them seemed to control anymore.

Chegwidden glanced once at the folder.

"You didn't cross town to tell me my coffee is bad."

"I might have. Principle matters."

"You would have sent a memo."

Fitzwallace set down his cup. "The President has a mission."

"For JAG?"

"For you. JAG is one reason."

Chegwidden drew the folder toward himself but did not open it until Fitzwallace nodded.

The brief began with maps.

The file started with geography—Sunnydale, California—then devolved into a litany of failures. Chegwidden’s pen paused over a list of hostile contacts, then moved to the section on fatalities hidden inside gang violence and missing-person reports. The category labels kept changing, as if the paper itself couldn't decide what kind of war this was. He kept his face neutral, a habit learned over decades of absorbing shocks.

"Local government compromised?" he asked, not looking up.

"Unknown extent. Assume leaks."

"Standing federal response?"

"None active."

Chegwidden flipped a page. The local response was a paragraph that read like a joke: patrols, a high-school library, a privately funded foreign national. He tapped the page. "This isn't a response team. It's a club."

"It's what they have," Fitzwallace said, his voice flat.

"Research support?"

"Two students and whatever books are in the library."

Chegwidden finally looked up from the folder, his expression hard. "That's unpaid intelligence analysis performed by the same people carrying the field mission. Who do they call when they need real assets?"

Fitzwallace’s silence was the answer. Chegwidden made a sharp note on his pad, the scratch of the pen loud in the quiet office.

Chegwidden turned a page. "Who has tactical authority?"

"The Slayer. Buffy Summers."

"Training?"

"Two years formal under the Watchers Council, active combat before and throughout."

"Prior service?"

"She's been the Slayer since she was fifteen."

Chegwidden stopped reading.

"Current age?"

Fitzwallace met his eyes. "Sixteen. Seventeen in January."

The admiral looked down at the casualty estimates again.

"You're telling me there's an active breach on American soil, and the entire standing response is a seventeen-year-old girl who buys her own bandages?"

"That's what I'm telling you."

Chegwidden closed the folder.

"All right. Start over. This time, tell me everything she doesn't have."

Fitzwallace did.

He described the absence of medicine, wages, logistics, legal protection, relief, records control, intelligence reach-back, and reliable backup. He described weapons stored in a school library and injuries explained to a mother who did not know vampires existed. He described the Oval conversation and Buffy's terms.

Chegwidden interrupted only to expose structure.

Chegwidden held up a hand, interrupting to expose the legal structure. "Does she request missions or accept them?"

"Neither. She identifies threats locally and acts."

"So we're a support element, not a command structure. Who defines a hostile?"

"She does."

"And if our intel sees a threat first?"

"We offer the intelligence. We don't launch our own hunt."

Chegwidden made a note. Jurisdiction. "Immediate mass casualty?"

"Standing exception. Clear and immediate threat."

He drew a box around the note. Boundaries. "Can she refuse personnel? Equipment?"

"Yes. Her call."

"And her pay is not tied to tasking. It's hazard pay, recognizing a condition of service, not a contract for it." He was outlining the charter in his head, a firewall of words. "Good. Now, what about the DRI?"

"Completely outside our chain."

"Good."

"You haven't read their file."

"I don't need to. Any organization that looks at this mission and begins with research instead of casualty reduction starts from the wrong verb."

Fitzwallace allowed himself the smallest smile. "That's why I'm here."

Chegwidden rose and walked to the map on his wall. "A team on the ground isn't a command. People use 'boots on the ground' when they mean the visible end of ten invisible systems. Operators need transport, communications, intelligence, medics, maintenance, supply, legal authority, a watch floor, and somebody awake at three in the morning who can move all of them."

"The President wants full command-and-control."

"Over our people."

"Only our people."

"Support role."

"Only. That does not mean passive. If we see something, we tell her. If we have an option, we offer it. If she asks for help, the answer cannot depend on whether the right clerk is awake."

Chegwidden turned. "If I take this, the charter protects her refusal in writing. Mission expansion comes back through me. No agency borrows the command as a collection platform. No doctor trades treatment for access. No one uses payroll to create an employment relationship she didn't accept."

"Agreed."

"I choose the personnel."

"Agreed."

"And if the White House wants a ceremonial task force that photographs well and cannot move a trauma patient at midnight, find another admiral."

"A.J., I came in person."

Chegwidden looked at his old friend. "Yes, you did."

He returned to the desk and opened a legal pad.

"I want every claimed authority the White House thinks applies, plus the opinions explaining why. I want the Summers file without psychiatric speculation presented as fact. I want the file on the officer you're considering for the advance element."

"Two personnel files," Fitzwallace said.

"I only named one."

"You said you choose the personnel. I brought options."

Fitzwallace removed two slimmer folders from his case.

Chegwidden read the names.

Lieutenant Jordan O'Neil.

Lieutenant Commander Lillian Urgayle.

He looked up. "Where does the President want headquarters?"

"You haven't asked."

"I asked for law and people first. Buildings are easy."

"Coronado."

Chegwidden nodded once. "That'll do."

"Is that a yes?"

"That's a conditional acceptance pending written authority."

"You became a lawyer."

"You noticed."

Fitzwallace stood to leave. Chegwidden came around the desk, stopping him with a hand on his shoulder. For a moment, they were just two old friends, the weight of the mission briefly set aside.

"Dinner," Chegwidden said. "Before another three years."

"You buying?"

"I'm making more coffee."

"Then I'll find a war to prevent."

They shook hands again.

By the time Fitzwallace reached the outer office, Chegwidden was reading the first personnel file.

✳

The map on the library table was a confession laid out in four layers of transparency and three colors of marker.

The base layer showed current streets. Over it she placed an 1899 municipal map Giles had found in the archive, then property lines copied from county records, then the sightings. Red dots marked displaced vampires and other predators. Blue traced Buffy's wrong shortcut and Angel's impossible scent trail.

Every concentration leaned against the same crooked line.

"It isn't a street," Buffy said.

"Not anymore," Willow replied. "It was the western town boundary before the 1911 annexation. The road moved, but the property descriptions kept pieces of it."

Angel studied the marks nearest Laurel Park. "The vampires weren't driven west by something chasing them. They reached the line and chose anything else."

"Choice implies intention," Giles said. "The pressure at the nest may have been a territorial warning."

"It implied a direction," Angel said. "Not a source."

Ethan stood at the end of the table, turning the silver shilling between his fingers. "A rule need not hate you to harm you. The city may be telling them where they are permitted to go."

"Cities don't tell people things," Buffy said.

"This one elected Richard Wilkins. Its judgment is already suspect."

Xander leaned over the map. The old boundary ran past the school, cut behind Restfield Cemetery, and crossed three residential blocks before vanishing under newer development.

He felt the pressure when his finger reached the cemetery.

"There."

Everyone looked at him.

"Same thing as the library. Not as strong."

Giles frowned. "You can sense it through the map?"

"I don't think it's the paper. More like remembering where it was made me notice where it is."

The library phone rang, making them all jump. Giles snatched it up. His posture stiffened as he listened, his free hand grabbing for a pencil. "Restfield," he bit out, scribbling the address. "Trapped, car crash... under attack."

Buffy didn't wait for the rest. She was already at the weapons chest, pulling out stakes.

Willow's eyes flew to the map. She jabbed a finger at the top transparency, her voice tight. "Giles, it's on the line. It's right on the line."

"They're concentrating the effect," Ethan said, his voice grim. "Forcing a response."

Xander felt the boundary half a block before he reached it.

Cold pressure rested against his chest, too broad to be a wall and too exact to be weather. Ahead, a station wagon had jumped the curb and folded its front end around a utility cabinet. Power lines spat blue light across the pavement. A woman and two children were trapped inside. Three vampires circled beyond the arcing cable, flinching from the electricity but not from the people.

"I feel it," Xander said.

"Where?" Buffy asked.

"Everywhere past that mailbox."

A vampire smashed the rear window of the station wagon.

The woman screamed.

"We don't have time to admire it," Buffy said. "Angel, left. I'm going through the yard. Willow, stay behind the corner and mark anything that repeats. Xander, get the car."

He crossed the line at a run.

For six strides the street behaved normally. On the seventh, the air tightened around him. On the eighth, he passed the same blue mailbox again.

Xander stopped.

Buffy had crossed through the yard and reached the nearest vampire. Angel hit the second from behind. The third was still tearing at the broken window.

Xander ran straight toward the car.

Mailbox. Cracked sidewalk. Dead lawn. Mailbox.

He poured on speed, the world dissolving into a gray smear around him.

The block blurred. The route did not. Each burst returned him to the same patch of pavement, momentum folded into destination without impact or turn. He was not bouncing off anything. Nothing resisted him. The city simply completed his movement where it had decided he belonged.

He launched upward.

Stars opened above the rooftops. For one second he cleared the power lines, the trees, the entire affected block.

Then his boots struck pavement beside the blue mailbox.

"What the—" He stopped, shook his head. "No, I don't even know what question to ask."

The vampire at the car pulled the door frame outward. Metal shrieked.

Xander's eyes heated.

There was no target. No wall. No visible field to burn. Heat vision crossed the street and struck exactly what it had been aimed at. It could destroy the mailbox, the pavement, the houses, or the vampire if Xander ever obtained a clear line.

It could not make over there become here.

"Stop pushing it!" Ethan shouted from behind the boundary.

Xander tried once more, faster.

The pressure tightened until the whole block seemed to twist around him. He returned to the mailbox hard enough to crater the asphalt.

"I noticed that part!"

Buffy drove her stake into the first vampire and rolled under the second one's swing. Angel reached the car, caught the vampire by its coat, and threw it away from the window.

"Victims first," Buffy ordered. "Angel, doors."

The electrical cable snapped against the road between them. Angel recoiled from the sudden arc.

The children screamed again.

Willow crouched behind the corner, her gaze darting between the chaos on the street and the map spread on the pavement. "The seal!" she yelled, her voice thin against the shriek of metal. "The mailbox—it has a city seal on it! The storm drain, too! They're...

"Anchors," Ethan finished, his eyes fixed on the unfolding scene. "The spell is using the city's authority as its anchor points."

Xander seized the mailbox post.

"Don't," Ethan snapped.

"It's one mailbox."

"And if breaking it transfers the rule to the person applying force?"

Xander let go.

"Strongly dislike magic."

"A promising beginning."

Giles arrived at a run carrying the archive map and a flashlight. He checked the stamped seal on the mailbox, then the mark cast into the storm drain.

"These are modern. The boundary predates both."

"Utility cabinet," Willow said.

"Installed in 1978."

Angel ripped the bent rear door from the station wagon. Buffy pulled the first child free and carried him through a narrow strip of yard the loop did not control.

The remaining vampires shifted together. They had learned that Xander could not reach them.

"They look happier," he said.

"Fix your geography," Buffy replied, "and I'll fix their attitude."

Willow traced the old line past the modern symbols. "They repeat because they're all city property. The spell is borrowing anything that says Sunnydale owns this spot."

Giles followed Willow's logic, sweeping his flashlight beam across the modern fixtures. "She's right. The spell is using official markers as a conduit." His light caught on the utility cabinet, then traveled higher, searching for something older. He brushed away a layer of soot above the cabinet's frame, revealing a round iron plate nearly swallowed by layers of paint.

"There," he breathed. "A survey marker. Nineteenth century. This isn't just a carrier. This is the original anchor."

The crashed car had driven the cabinet sideways. Live current crawled across the iron housing and the flooded gutter beneath it. The old marker was wedged between the cabinet and a cracked masonry pillar. Any ordinary person trying to reach it would cross both the arcing line and the unstable weight of the vehicle.

"Can I break that one?" Xander asked.

Ethan examined the mark from beyond the line. "No. It has been instructed to hold the boundary while it remains in the city's keeping."

"Meaning?"

Willow saw it first. "Don't break it. Take it. If the city doesn't have it anymore, it can't hold anything."

"Ownership magic," Ethan said. "Appalling, but yes."

Giles pointed to the four old bolts. "Remove it intact. Do not damage the seal."

Buffy drew both vampires away from the car, moving backward through the yard. Angel carried the woman. The live cable fell across the only direct route to the marker.

Xander walked through it.

Blue-white current seized him, turning the air to ozone. His jacket crisped to ash and every fuse in the cabinet blew with a series of sharp cracks, but the voltage found nothing to burn on his skin. Bracing the crushed station wagon with one shoulder, he reached into the charged space between steel and masonry and began turning the rusted bolts, his touch careful enough not to shear them.

The pressure tried to return him to the mailbox.

He did not cross the boundary. He worked from the place it permitted him to occupy, following the rule instead of fighting its destination.

The final bolt came free.

Xander lifted the iron plate from its setting.

"This belongs to me now," he said, his voice cutting through the hum of the city.

The block released him.

There was no flash. The street simply became a street again.

He crossed it before the vampires understood the difference.

Buffy staked one. Xander caught the other by the back of its coat when it tried to run and held it at arm's length.

"Her attitude adjustment is more permanent than mine," he said.

The vampire looked at Buffy and stopped struggling.

"Smart," Buffy said, and dusted it.

Willow came out from behind the corner. Giles checked the rescued family. Angel pulled the power cable away from the wet road.

Xander held up the soot-blackened survey plate.

"We won?"

Ethan looked at the modern city seals, the old boundary, and each place their attention had gone.

"Yes," he said. "Which is not quite the comfort you think it is."

✳

Evelyn Rook watched the survey plate leave municipal possession.

The corresponding line on her working faded. She noted the time, the method, and the six distinct decisions which had produced it.

She traced the sequence of the failure. Harris flinched from a line he couldn't see. Rayne pulled him back from a blind, forceful attack. Giles, the Watcher, found the anchor in some dusty municipal history. It was the Rosenberg girl, however, who had leaped from 'break it' to 'take it,' identifying the working's dependence on ownership. The vampire confirmed it, and Summers managed the entire field, protecting the civilians as if it were second nature.

Holland read the list over her shoulder. "Your boundary failed."

"The boundary was removed."

"That distinction may be important in your department."

"It is important in every department. Execution has not begun."

Rook placed the modern carriers in one column and drew a line through them. Exposed anchors would not survive this group. Neither would a working which assumed Harris attacked alone.

"What did begin?" Holland asked.

"Measurement."

She recorded their response times. Summers had established command in under four seconds. Rosenberg had identified repetition in forty-three. Giles found the historical distinction in one minute, eleven seconds. Rayne had prevented the two destructive actions most likely to trigger a transfer clause.

"They cooperate," Holland said.

"Most people cooperate. They defer. That is more useful."

"To the Slayer?"

"According to domain. Combat to Summers. Interpretation to Giles. Ritual risk to Rayne. Structural inference to Rosenberg. Mr. Harris did not enjoy waiting, but he obeyed information he could not verify himself."

Holland looked at the blank space between the two headings in her file. "How does that help separate him?"

"It tells me not to offer them an object they can remove together. The next components will be records, consent, and identity."

She wrote a request on firm letterhead.

She would need his entire civic existence. School records, to see how he was disciplined. Residence history, to map his life inside the city. Every signature he'd ever put on a hall pass or a library card. She needed to define Alexander Harris, the boy, so thoroughly that the city itself would refuse to recognize the man he'd become.

"For Wilkins?" Holland asked.

"Through him. The principal already understands requests from the Mayor."

"And if Mr. Harris notices?"

"He will. Eventually."

Rook closed the field log.

The failure belonged to the boundary.

Everything it had learned belonged to her.

✳

The watch began its urgent pattern while Giles was wrapping the iron survey plate in three layers of cloth.

One long pulse. Pause. Again. Again.

Xander looked at the cracked utility map, the residue samples, and the people who had just proved he needed all of them to cross one city block.

"Of course," he said.

Giles handed him the telephone.

The secure contact answered on the second ring. "Requesting government is the People's Republic of China."

China. Of course.

"Seismic event in western Gansu," the voice continued, clinical and distant. "Landslide has buried portions of two villages and cut the primary rescue road. We have confirmed survivors trapped under a collapsed school and a textile workshop."

Xander's anger at the timing disappeared beneath the word school.

"Authorization?"

"Formal authorization received for a narrow flight corridor and operation inside the disaster district. Local incident commander is Chief Engineer Lin Wei. English-speaking liaison will meet you at the southern staging area."

"Any reason not to trust it?"

The officer paused. "Diplomatic relations remain strained. We have no indication the disaster or request is false. Acceptance remains your decision."

The people under the buildings had made no diplomatic decisions at all.

"I'm going."

He changed in Giles's office, switched off the civilian watch, and activated the receiver hidden inside the costume. Buffy watched the blue uniform settle across his shoulders.

"You don't have to prove anything after tonight," she said.

"Kids under a school."

"I know. Keep the receiver on."

"I will."

He launched from the library roof.

The flight across the Pacific gave him too much time to remember the blue mailbox. He flew faster than sound over empty water and felt no resistance, no folding route, no city deciding where his movement ended. Ocean became coastline exactly when distance said it should.

Physical space had never felt so reassuring.

The disaster district lay in a mountain valley under a brown veil of dust. Whole slopes had collapsed. Roofs emerged from mud at wrong angles. Hundreds of civilians worked beside soldiers and rescue crews, passing baskets of debris hand to hand where vehicles could not reach.

Xander landed at the marked southern staging area slowly enough not to add wind to the dust.

A thin man in an orange rescue helmet approached with an interpreter and a PLA liaison. The man ignored the costume and pointed immediately toward the slope.

"Chief Engineer Lin," the interpreter said. "He says the school first. Secondary collapse expected. Twenty-three children, four adults unaccounted for."

"Show me where to lift."

Lin did.

The school roof had pancaked across two classrooms. Xander could have torn through from above, but Lin stopped him and marked three load paths which still protected air pockets. Xander held a concrete beam while crews tunneled beneath it. He lifted an entire corner of the roof six inches, no more, until shoring went into place. He listened through stone for heartbeats and tapped locations for the rescue teams rather than ripping toward every sound.

They brought out nine children in the first twenty minutes.

One small girl clung to his cape until a medic took her. Xander returned beneath the slab before anyone could photograph her face.

At the textile workshop, industrial machinery had trapped workers against a failing retaining wall. Lin showed him where the slope would move if he lifted the wrong section. Xander carried two presses out one at a time, opened an access route, and held back mud while conventional crews recovered the injured.

The rescue was real.

So was the gratitude. Villagers touched his hands. A grandmother pressed a dust-covered red ribbon into his palm and would not take it back. Lin shook his hand after the last confirmed survivor reached the triage tents.

"He says your help saved thirty-one people," the interpreter translated.

"Tell him his people told me how."

Lin understood enough English to answer himself. "You listened."

"I learned that recently."

The PLA liaison had stayed close all afternoon. Major Qiao spoke excellent English and had provided maps, frequencies, and no operational interference. Only after Lin released Xander did Qiao point toward a military convoy waiting beyond the civilian staging area.

"Your authorized departure corridor has shifted because of air-traffic restrictions," he said. "We will transport you to the control facility. There will be a short debrief while clearance is confirmed."

"I don't need transportation."

"The corridor begins at the facility. Your government has requested compliance with authorized routing."

Xander touched the receiver beneath the uniform. Static answered. The mountains and damaged communications had produced intermittent contact all day.

He looked back at the rescue site. The last thing he wanted was an argument near injured civilians.

"Short debrief," he said.

The convoy took twelve minutes to reach the airfield.

The facility had been built partly into the mountain. Its exterior resembled an emergency coordination bunker: broad vehicle doors, communications masts, personnel moving around relief trucks. Qiao led him through two checkpoints into a concrete room containing a table, cameras, and no windows.

The door closed behind him.

Metal locks drove into place above, below, and on both sides.

Xander turned.

"That sounded less like a debrief door."

White vapor poured from vents near the ceiling.

Qiao had remained outside.

A voice came through a speaker. "You will remain calm. The agent is not harmful."

Xander held his breath, then remembered he did not have to. He inhaled deliberately. The vapor smelled sweet and medicinal. It did nothing.

Panels opened in the walls.

Conductive cables fired from four directions, wrapped his arms and torso, and discharged enough current to turn the air blue. A weighted net dropped from above. Anchors hammered into the floor as winches pulled every restraint tight.

The table shattered beneath him.

He remained standing.

"Superman," the voice said, "you will submit to non-destructive examination. Your status and future cooperation will then be negotiated."

Xander looked down at the smoking cables.

Earlier, he had flown straight toward a station wagon and arrived beside a mailbox. He had accelerated, climbed, burned the air, and found no argument the city was required to accept.

This was wire.

This was steel.

This was a room built by people who thought enough material could become a rule.

"No," he said.

The winches increased tension.

"You entered Chinese territory under authorization. You are subject to necessary security procedures."

"Authorization to rescue people is not authorization to kidnap me."

"You will not be harmed."

"Open the door."

The answer was another electrical discharge.

Something in Xander stopped trying to be reasonable.

He moved his arms apart.

The restraint cables snapped. Two anchors came out of the reinforced floor in chunks of concrete. The net tore down the middle and fell around his boots.

Alarms began.

Xander looked through the walls.

Thirty-seven people occupied the surrounding bunker. Eight in the control room beyond the north wall. Six soldiers in the corridor. Four more approaching from the vehicle bay. Nobody stood directly behind the western wall.

He went west.

Concrete did not explode so much as cease agreeing that it was a wall. Xander drove through reinforcing bars, layered ceramic armor, a cavity filled with impact foam, and a second concrete shell. Dust and broken steel filled the chamber behind him.

He emerged into an equipment room.

Mechanical arms dropped from the ceiling. He caught one, tore it free, and used it to sweep the others into a bank of sensors. Warning lights burst. A rack of recording equipment toppled in sparks.

Soldiers appeared at the far door and raised weapons.

Xander saw their fingers, their breathing, the fear widening their eyes.

He did not touch them.

He tore the armored door out of the wall and threw it through the unmanned capture rig behind him.

"Move," he said.

They moved.

The next corridor ended at a blast door thick enough to stop an aircraft engine. A red light came on above it. Gas jets opened from both walls.

Xander did not look for controls.

He put both hands against the center seam and pulled.

The locking bars held for less than a second. The door folded outward, ripped from its upper track, and crashed flat into the empty corridor beyond. Xander walked over it.
