"I want to speak to my daughter alone."

"Of course."

He reached for his coat.

"Tomorrow," Joyce said. "You come back tomorrow and tell me everything about this Council. Who they are, who pays you, what authority they think they have over Buffy, and why none of them ever came through that door."

Giles met Buffy's eyes once. "I will."

"And bring records. Not books about prophecy. Records about her."

Giles paused, his hand halfway to his coat. The Council had histories of Slayers, field reports, prophecies, and training texts. Whether it possessed anything which treated Buffy Summers as a child with a home was another question.

"Everything I can obtain," Giles said.

After he left, Joyce checked the lock. She looked through the front window at the last federal vehicle disappearing from the street.

"Can vampires break that?"

"The lock? Yes. The threshold matters more. If you don't invite them, they can't enter."

"I invited Darla."

"You didn't know."

"Because you didn't tell me."

Buffy recoiled.

Joyce closed her eyes. "No. That's not fair. You did tell me."

"Once."

"Once should have been enough for me to ask better questions."

"What question would have gotten you to vampires?"

"I don't know."

"That's the problem."

Joyce turned from the window. "Are you angry with me?"

Buffy made a sound that was almost a laugh, short and flat. "I don't know how to answer that tonight."

"You can say yes."

"Yes. I'm angry. I'm also tired and scared you're going to wake up tomorrow and decide President Bartlet was part of a nervous breakdown. I'm waiting for you to tell me I can't leave the house again. I'm waiting for the part where knowing makes everything worse."

"I want to tell you that you can't leave the house."

"I know."

"You're sixteen. You just told me you can be killed. What am I supposed to say? Have a good patrol?"

"You can say take the phone they're giving me. You can learn the rules. You can help me get to a doctor when I need one. You can stop making me invent a different lie every time I come home bleeding."

"And I let you go?"

"Being the Slayer isn't up to you. Whether I have a mother to come back to—that is."

Joyce absorbed the sentence without defense.

"I don't know how to do this," she said.

"Neither do I."

"You've been doing it for two years."

"Not this part."

Joyce moved toward her, then stopped before assuming an embrace was wanted. Buffy saw the hesitation. For a moment neither knew whether asking would make the distance worse.

Buffy crossed it herself.

Joyce held her carefully, as if her daughter's extraordinary strength had somehow made her more fragile. Buffy allowed it for several breaths. She did not say forgiveness. Joyce did not ask.

When they separated, Joyce looked at the tear beneath Buffy's arm.

"Is that from tonight?"

"Last night. Tire iron."

"Let me see."

Buffy removed the coat and raised her sleeve. The bruise had spread around her shoulder.

Joyce's first instinct was outrage that anyone had struck her child. Her second was to reach for the household first-aid kit. Both were inadequate. For the first time, she knew they were inadequate.

"Does it need a doctor?"

"Probably not. Giles says rest."

"Will you?"

"Tonight, yes."

That small answer was the first thing knowledge changed.

Joyce brought ice and a towel. Buffy explained invitation rules while her mother held the pack against her shoulder. They made a list of names to call, what Joyce should never promise under threat, and where Buffy kept the stakes.

It was not reconciliation.

It was planning.

✳

Principal Snyder delivered the school file to the Mayor personally.

He did not ask why Richard Wilkins required Alexander Harris's elementary enrollment records, original registration signature, attendance history, disciplinary reports, yearbook photographs, and current emergency-contact card. He did point out that the file documented years of behavior which should have justified expulsion long before now.

"Persistent disruption," Snyder said. "Defiance of authority. Unexplained absences. Association with Summers."

Wilkins turned past a report from the sixth grade. "That certainly does sound like a pattern."

"If this concerns legal action, the school district will support any reasonable measure."

"Nothing so unpleasant. We're making sure young Alexander receives the civic attention he deserves."

Snyder smiled. "Good."

He left without noticing the woman in gray who entered through the side door after him.

Evelyn Rook wore gloves to handle the school file. Not because paper was dangerous, but because careless practitioners supplied sympathetic material while collecting it.

"This is incomplete," she said.

Wilkins's smile remained. "Our principal has been very thorough."

"The school has. A school is not a city."

On the conference table she arranged the records by relationship rather than date.

Birth registration established the name. School forms established repeated voluntary use. Attendance records described where municipal authority expected him to be. Disciplinary entries showed recognition under that name even in conflict. Photographs tied the social face across time. Emergency contacts connected him to a household. Library slips, vaccination records, and a Little League roster added smaller agreements made by institutions which had no idea agreement mattered.

City clerks and county registrars supplied the rest. Residence history. Utility service at the Harris address. Property boundaries. Tax parcels. Census entries. The incorporation map which declared the house inside Sunnydale, and decades of civic records which continued acting as though that declaration were true.

Wilkins picked up a yearbook photograph. Twelve-year-old Xander faced the camera with a smile already prepared to become a joke if anyone looked too closely.

"His name is hardly secret," Wilkins said. "Half the world has seen his face by now."

"A name is a label. I require the web which makes the labeled person legible to this jurisdiction."

"That sounds time-consuming."

"It is less time-consuming than discovering halfway through a working that a school nickname, absent father, or disputed address gives the subject grounds to reject recognition."

"Superman broke a fortified military installation yesterday."

"Yes."

"I would prefer not to see him test City Hall next."

Rook took a press photograph from a separate folder. Superman hovered above the Antarctic ice, face clear, cape pulled sideways by the wind.

"China proved my point. Physical barriers are an invitation to a creature like that. He understands steel, distance, and heat. Confine him with walls, and he simply treats them as an engineering problem to be solved with force.

"And your route held him."

"Because it did not oppose departure. It defined the destination."

She placed the Superman photograph beside Xander's school picture.

The faces were the same. The records were not.

Alexander Lavelle Harris had a birth, a lawful residence, a school, a mother and father of record, years of municipal contact, and an accepted place inside Sunnydale's boundary.

Superman had a face, public acts, diplomatic controversy, and international reports. He had no birth certificate, no address, no sovereign employer, no office recognized by law, and no declared residence inside Sunnydale.

"They're one person," Wilkins said.

"Physically. But legally, they are two separate stories. Our work begins where those stories contradict."

"How soon?"

Rook looked at him over the photographs. "You purchased a completed service, Mr. Mayor. You did not purchase control of my practice."

His smile cooled by less than a degree. "Naturally. I only mention it because civic calendars can be so unforgiving."

"Then provide the exhaustive schedule of protected obligations I requested. The working cannot preserve obligations you refuse to define."

"You'll have it."

"When I do, I will proceed."

Rook returned the photographs to their separate files.

Rook filed the photographs, separating one identity from the other. The theory was now ready for practice.

✳

The command acquired a watch floor at 0600 the next morning.

Six telephones sat in a neat row beside four secure terminals. On the wall, two large maps were already pinned with incident markers. A duty officer and three analysts watched the screens, their quiet interrupted only when Urgayle scowled at the coffee maker, declaring it hostile on first contact.

Chegwidden considered that sufficient to begin issuing standing orders.

"Twenty-four-hour coverage starts now," he told the section leads. "If Summers calls, a cleared person answers. Not a recording, not a switchboard that requests a project code she has never heard, and not an officer who tells her to call during working hours."

Communications assigned the number and paired it to a handset ready for delivery. A redundant pager channel and Giles's secure library line remained available until they proved the handset worked through Sunnydale's anomalies.

"Intelligence fusion begins with existing records," Chegwidden continued. "Police incidents, unexplained deaths, missing persons, hospital patterns, animal attacks, fires, municipal disruptions, and whatever local categories have been used to conceal supernatural contact. Research integrates historical and archaeological sources. Dr. Jackson is specialist reach-back, not the entire research plan."

The intelligence lead raised one hand. "Collection boundaries?"

"Still apply when curiosity becomes inconvenient. Summers and her associates are partners and sources, not targets. If information about them is incidentally collected, protect it. If you want intentional collection, bring me an independently lawful reason."

Medical had located a cleared trauma physician at Naval Medical Center San Diego and begun emergency privileges for a civilian clinic closer to Sunnydale. A protected patient number would prevent ordinary billing disclosure. A helicopter could reach a pre-surveyed pickup zone once the local team selected it. Ground evacuation remained primary when magic, weather, or secrecy made aircraft unwise.

"Limitations?" Chegwidden asked.

"No cleared surgeon in Sunnydale yet. Blood supply would come through San Diego. We cannot promise confidentiality from an ordinary emergency room if she arrives outside the protected route."

"Put all three in the briefing."

Logistics had packed replacement clothing, boots, conventional first-aid supplies, stakes, blessed water containers, evidence packaging, portable lights, two secure lockers, and radios. Crossbow bolts were delayed while counsel determined whether their intended use created a procurement category nobody wanted to name.

Urgayle said, "Buy sporting bolts. Modify locally after delivery."

"Legal?" Chegwidden asked.

The lawyer considered it. "Permissible if the modification is documented and the final equipment stays inside the compartment."

"Done."

The finance officer slid a single sheet of paper to the center of the table. "I had twelve pages of justification," he said. "This is faster.

"Classified civil-defense trust," he said. "The initial deposit compensates prior hazardous service. Continuing payments are monthly and independent of patrols, contacts, kills, missions accepted, or compliance with tasking. Summers is beneficiary and controls ordinary spending. Because she is a minor, the account technically requires an adult custodian, but counsel has structured the custodian's role as fiduciary only. No authority to redirect funds or condition access."

"Who is the custodian?" O'Neil asked.

"Joyce Summers, if she accepts after independent explanation. Alternate is a cleared federal fiduciary chosen from three options presented to Buffy."

"Usable?"

The finance officer placed a temporary bank card and a packet of checks on the table. "Today. The account is funded."

Hazard pay had become money before the command possessed a sign on its door.

Chegwidden moved to transport. Two ordinary vehicles would support the advance element without advertising a military presence. One armored medical vehicle remained at Coronado unless requested. Rotary and fixed-wing support stayed on call. Every operation required independent departure planning, continuous contact, and an external watch officer empowered to react when terms changed.

"Sunnydale advance element," he said.

O'Neil stood beside a roster reduced from twenty-seven names to nine.

"Initial entry is four people. Me, Master Chief Urgayle, a communications specialist, and a medic. No tactical team until Buffy receives the offer and agrees there is a role. The others establish reach-back here."

Urgayle had chosen field personnel who could work around civilians, adolescents, and unknown nonhumans without treating ambiguity as permission to fire. Two qualified operators had been removed after referring to Sunnydale as a target environment. One physician had been removed after asking when Slayer tissue samples would become available.

"Evidence storage?" Chegwidden asked.

"Temporary secure lockers ready," logistics said. "Permanent site unresolved."

"Cleanup?"

"Protective gear and a contractor cover mechanism ready. No validated procedure for supernatural biological residue."

"Research?"

"Municipal and county requests drafted. Archives require local negotiation. No complete demon taxonomy exists, and sources conflict."

"Good."

The section leads had learned what he meant by then. Gaps stated plainly. No flags planted in uncertainty.

O'Neil asked, "When do we brief Buffy?"

"Today. Before one person enters her town under this mission."

"And the presidential incident?"

"Is not precedent for entry without notice. It is an example we will all work very hard not to repeat."

Chegwidden signed the first standing orders.

At the top, in language no later commander could mistake for a suggestion, the mission began with one verb.

Support.

✳

Joyce did not sleep.

At two in the morning she knocked on Buffy's bedroom door and asked where the stakes were kept. Buffy showed her. At three, Joyce asked whether every invitation counted or only one given at a home. At four, she asked what sunlight did, whether crosses required belief, and why the windows did not have locks strong enough to stop anything Buffy had described.

At five, she asked whether Buffy wanted her there.

Buffy took longer to answer that one.

"I don't know yet," she said. "I want you to know. Those aren't the same thing."

Joyce accepted the answer because accepting it was the first useful thing she could do.

She called the gallery and said she would not be in. She called the school and reported Buffy ill without asking whether a night spent revealing vampires qualified. Then she stood in the kitchen for twenty minutes doing nothing she could afterward account for.

O'Neil called at seven thirty through the secure number left the night before. She explained that Buffy's hazard-pay account had been approved and funded, and that finance needed an adult fiduciary because Buffy was a minor.

Joyce's first response was anger. "You want to pay my daughter to fight monsters."

"No," O'Neil said. "Payment is not per fight. It is compensation for hazardous service she is already performing and has performed without support. It cannot be withheld because she refuses a mission, person, or piece of equipment."

"And you want me to control it."

"The fiduciary can protect the account but cannot redirect the money or condition Buffy's access. You would have legal duties, not ownership. Buffy can choose a cleared independent fiduciary instead."

Joyce looked across the table at her daughter.

The old instinct was to decide which option protected Buffy best, then announce it as a parental fact. After the night they had survived, Joyce heard the danger inside that instinct.

"It's her choice," she said.

Buffy looked up.

"Yes," O'Neil replied. "We'll explain both options at the briefing. The account is usable today either way."

After the call, Buffy touched the temporary card inside the delivery packet as if money could be more impossible than vampires.

"Hazard pay," Joyce said.

"Apparently."

Joyce had always understood 'hazard pay' as a warning. A reason to stop. For the first time, she heard it as a price. An invoice for services already rendered, submitted by the United States government for the piece of her daughter's life they now considered a line item.

Xander arrived at Giles's apartment after sunrise. The Secret Service had released him from questions shortly after midnight, O'Neil had conducted another rescue-protocol interview at one, and Snyder still expected him in school.

Ethan was awake at the small dining table, the old municipal map spread beneath the iron survey marker.

"You look dreadful," he said.

"Apparently abducting a consenting President is tiring."

"A sentence with admirable ambition."

"He says it wasn't abduction."

"Of course he does."

In Los Angeles, Rook began the identity working.

The room contained no photographs of Superman. The public identity was absence, and absence required no sympathetic anchor yet.

Alexander Harris required many.

She set his original school registration beneath a sheet of prepared vellum. His municipal residence record went to the north, birth registration to the east, attendance history to the west, and the 1841 territorial description beneath them all. A line of ink connected household to parcel, parcel to town, town to name.

Rook wrote slowly.

Alexander.

At Giles's table, Xander looked toward the window.

"Did you feel that?"

Ethan was already reaching for chalk.

Rook continued.

Lavelle.

The sensation closed around Xander without touching his skin. It was not cold this time. It felt like standing in a crowd and hearing a stranger speak his name from directly behind him.

He turned.

Nobody was there.

Harris.

The town noticed him.

Not Xander as Buffy knew him, Willow teased him, or Giles had taught him. Not the body wearing Superman's face. It noticed the person on school forms, utility records, maps, and disciplinary reports. Every small official agreement that he belonged here pulled in the same direction.

For one second, he felt more precisely located than he had ever been in his life.

Then the pressure vanished.

"What was that?"

Ethan drew a broken circle around Xander's chair, tested the air, and stood there a moment with his jaw tight.

"Your name has been placed under a rule."

Xander stood. "Where?"

"If I knew, I would have begun with that information."

"Can we track it?"

"Not yet. It came through relationships the city already accepts. Residence, record, perhaps blood. Destroying the object nearest you would be gratifying and useless."

"So what do we do?"

"Identify which facts the working has invoked. Your full name, certainly. Likely your address and the town boundary. We find places where those facts disagree, records that changed, jurisdictions that overlap. Rules built from identity are strongest when everyone accepts the same identity without question."

"Everybody does accept my name."

Ethan gave him a dry look. "You have spent the last week insisting that several billion people call you Superman, while several others absolutely must not. 'Uncomplicated' is not the term I would choose for your present relationship with names.

"Can I stop it by telling people?"

"Do not confuse identifying a tension with prescribing a cure. Disclosure may weaken the distinction, strengthen the public identity, or provide the caster a voluntary declaration she currently lacks. We investigate before you hand a professional sorcerer additional facts."

"And if we can't find the rule?"

"Then we learn what it permits before testing what it resists. Precisely as we practiced."

"What if I find the caster?"

"Then you will remember the caster has studied what happens when you become angry at a structure."

Xander looked down at the map. His street appeared as one thin line among hundreds.

At Wolfram & Hart, the ink dried.

Under ALEXANDER LAVELLE HARRIS, Rook connected the name to a lawful address, a school, a municipal parcel, and uninterrupted residence inside the jurisdiction.

Under SUPERMAN, she placed nothing.

No address.

No birth.

No accepted office.

No lawful claim of residence inside Sunnydale.

In Coronado, Chegwidden's standing orders built authority around the right to refuse. Communications, medicine, money, transport, research, and force were being connected so none could quietly become ownership.

In Los Angeles, Rook used the same tools of definition for the opposite purpose.

She drew the final line between resident and foreign power.

The working recognized Xander.

It did not recognize Superman.

✳

In Sunnydale, Giles spread the revised threat map across the library table. It showed ports, airfields, power corridors, hospitals, and the places where panic would move faster than an army.

“We hold the ground response,” Giles said. “Not because Xander might arrive. Because civilians will be here whether he does or not.”

Willow layered the ward grid over the evacuation routes. Ethan marked the seams where hostile magic could turn a road, a shelter, or a crowd into an anchor. They built plans with Xander, without him, and for when the enemy learned the difference.

✳

Chapter 23: The City Line

Scene 1

The advance element arrived in the parking lot of a Sunnydale strip mall that advertised psychic readings, tax preparation, and all-you-can-eat Chinese. Chegwidden, O’Neil, and Urgayle wore civilian jackets over uniforms that could have been anything from government-issue to the discount rack at Sears. The rented SUV still smelled of plastic and low-mileage shame. Urgayle said nothing about the paint match between the Suburban and the faded curb stripes, but his scowl measured the tactical possibilities of each parked car.

Sunnydale did not notice. Sunnydale never noticed.

They made a slow circuit before entering the office space the Navy had leased on forty-eight hours’ notice. It came furnished with an ancient copy machine, two plastic palm trees, and a conference table sticky from the last tenant’s enthusiasm for both donuts and litigation. Chegwidden surveyed the windowless room, said “Better than half the places I’ve commanded from,” and set his briefcase in the exact center.

O’Neil examined the security panel. “Main entrance is glass, deadbolt’s civilian grade. Two internal lines already installed, but the comms team needs another hour for full secure. Copier is a bust.”

“Define bust,” Chegwidden said.

“Tonally matched to the coffee, sir.”

Urgayle had brought three bags: one for the comms gear, one for logistics, and one for the field medicine. He unpacked nothing, surveyed the perimeter, and assigned the first two civilian security specialists their posts. The third was still en route; Urgayle left her a note in the form of a black watch cap and an open energy bar.

They ran first protocols on whiteboards stripped of any branding.

Buffy and Giles arrived at the back door seven minutes early. Giles still had the aura of a man who believed the official world could be tamed by tweed and patience, but the dark lenses he now wore told another story. Buffy wore a jacket with new seams under one arm, and the look of a person grading every grown-up she met for any sign of liar or zealot.

Xander came two minutes later, dressed as Alexander Harris in every way except for the loose-fitting gloves that would not have passed at the Army-Navy surplus. He had a manila folder under one arm, a school ID in his back pocket, and the posture of a demolition expert waiting for the all-clear. Willow, apparently, had been left in reserve.

Chegwidden invited them into the main room without a handshake. He introduced himself, the officers, and the mission in the language of arson investigation: “My job is to prevent secondary fires. Everything else, including us, is containment.”

He gestured at the table. “Slayer at the head. Mr. Harris, next. Giles, you may float.”

Buffy raised an eyebrow, but took the head of the table without comment.

Chegwidden began. “We are here to provide infrastructure, not management. That means our orders are to support, not to command. I am required to say that out loud at the start of every meeting until you believe it.”

“How many meetings are you planning?” Xander said.

“Enough that you can break the schedule with a phone call, but not enough to become the next problem.”

O’Neil ran through the briefing. Each offer was listed by category: medical, communications, logistics, research, and emergency extraction. Each had a clause for voluntary refusal, with the refusals to be logged but not second-guessed.

“Refusing help doesn’t end the help,” O’Neil said. “It just means we try again with a different approach.”

Giles glanced at the research annex. “Will you expect reciprocal access to Watcher records?”

“Offer only,” O’Neil said. “We will not request the Council’s proprietary material, but if you choose to share a lead, we’ll treat it as privileged. The trade is: you get a Navy research assistant and all the open source we can scrape, and we never ask where you got your own.”

Buffy said, “What happens if you see something before we do?”

Urgayle answered. “We inform you. We offer resources. If you decline, we observe. If lives are at risk, and only if, we act independently—but only after making the refusal clear and logging it. You get the next review, and you can appeal every action up the chain.”

Giles looked at the appeals flowchart. “I see Admiral Chegwidden is the top of the chain.”

“I picked the job,” Chegwidden said. “If I’m not the right adult for the appeal, you’ll know immediately.”

Xander flipped through his own copy of the protocol sheet. “Medical support says ‘independent physician, no sample retention, no research without specific consent.’ Does that include blood draws after a... situation?”

O’Neil did not smile. “Only for treatment. Not a research sample unless you sign a fresh form every time. The base has already shredded a dozen requests from the Science Directorate to use you for baseline studies.”

“They’re relentless,” Xander said.

“Yes, but not our problem,” O’Neil said.

Buffy scanned the rescue section. “If I get hit and you have to evac me, is my name in every Navy database by the next morning?”

“Patient identity is protected. You have a case number, not a record. If you want it wiped after treatment, you can request deletion. If you want it shared with anyone, you can direct that too. But until then, it’s sealed above most military secrets.”

Buffy closed her copy. “And if you get a direct order from higher up? White House, Pentagon?”

“Admiral Chegwidden takes the call,” O’Neil said. “We go through him or not at all.”

Chegwidden waited for the challenge.

It came from Xander. “What’s the catch? You don’t field this much support unless somebody’s expecting a mess that can’t be solved with a phone call and a nice letter.”

Chegwidden’s expression shifted half a degree. “The catch is you are already operating at a level that would require three special-ops teams, five intelligence shops, and a support battalion to keep up. The world can only pretend to ignore that for so long. My job is to make the world never notice unless you want it to.”

Buffy folded her arms. “So what happens if we say no?”

“Then you continue as before, but with better phone numbers and a Navy base willing to answer them at 3 a.m. If you want the base to leave, the base leaves.”

“Do you believe that?” she asked Xander.

He studied the faces at the table. “I believe that if we say go, Chegwidden makes it happen. I also believe the first time I punch a hole through something with ‘classified’ on the side, half the world’s going to notice and the other half will want to own me.”

Giles nodded. “That sounds plausible.”

O’Neil passed a copy of the field medical form to Buffy. “Try not to need this, but if you do, the medic will ask only what you want recorded. None of this gets to your mother or anyone else unless you approve it.”

Buffy met O’Neil’s gaze. “You’re not what I expected.”

“Neither are you,” O’Neil replied.

Urgayle interrupted, his tone formal. “Three minutes to secure the exterior.”

Chegwidden closed his folder. “That is your cue to see if the team can move through Sunnydale without waking the whole town. First test: show them the park, the school, and anything you consider important. Not the Hellmouth, not yet.”

Buffy exchanged looks with Xander. “We taking them on patrol?”

“That’s the idea,” Chegwidden said.

Buffy rose, buttoned her jacket, and beckoned Xander to her side. Giles followed, already reviewing the field notes and muttering about procurement rules for crossbow bolts. O’Neil and Urgayle followed at an unobtrusive distance, their posture somewhere between personal security and very polite observation.

Outside, the town looked unchanged, but the air had the weight of secrets waiting to tip.

They walked the route past the park, under the half-lit lamplight of a playground that doubled as both a supernatural hot zone and a place for toddlers to experiment with gravity. Buffy explained the difference between normal and not-normal vampire behavior. O’Neil asked if the park had ever been used as a muster point by the opposition. Xander said, “Every week. They’re as unoriginal as high-school vandals and twice as lazy. If they were smart, they’d use the mall.”

Urgayle took silent notes on a small, waterproof pad.

When the route swung by the school, Xander hesitated at the sight of the blue mailbox. The repaired sidewalk still bore a half-moon scar from his last encounter. O’Neil noticed, but did not ask.

They passed through the cemetery, where Angel waited. He wore the expression of someone who had arrived early and spent the time inventing reasons to regret it. He nodded at Buffy, ignored O’Neil, and gave Urgayle a look that suggested he had already memorized the new team’s gait, scent, and probable intentions.

O’Neil surprised everyone by asking Angel to brief her on Sunnydale’s post-midnight traffic patterns.

“Your people use patrol routes,” she said. “So do the others. How often do they repeat?”

“Too often,” Angel said.

“Do they avoid surveillance or approach it?”

“Depends who’s in town. The smarter ones use the sewers or split up before anyone can follow.”

“Any rules about who gets first pick of new ground?”

Angel gave her a flat look. “Nobody’s written them down, but yeah. They don’t like a crowd unless they’re hunting. If they’re moving in numbers, there’s a reason.”

O’Neil scribbled on her pad. “Thank you.”

The conversation moved to surface level again. They completed the round trip in under forty minutes, never once drawing the attention of a police cruiser, a wandering night jogger, or any of the three actual vampires Xander saw shadowing them from the far side of the street. It felt like a test, and the town passed.

Back at the base, Chegwidden had arranged coffee, disposable cups, and what might have been a donut had it not lost its will to live somewhere near Bakersfield. He gestured everyone back to the table.

“Now you know the field,” he said. “What do you want to change?”

Buffy answered first. “Don’t trail us. Don’t shadow the cemeteries unless we call. And if you see something, say something, but don’t try to ‘intervene’ without a reason you can explain in one sentence.”

Chegwidden wrote that down, word for word.

“Mr. Harris?” he prompted.

“Keep the science team off my back,” Xander said. “If they need a sample, they get it from the hardware, not the software. And if you have a plan for what to do if I snap, you tell me about it. I don’t want a secret fail-safe nobody can talk about.”

“Done,” Chegwidden said.

“Giles?” O’Neil asked.

“Procurement forms,” Giles said. “Less than four pages, please.”

O’Neil gave a half-smile. “We’ll do our best.”

The final protocol was signed. The command was functional. The offer—support, not command—was real.

Buffy took the first donut and broke it in half. She passed one half to Xander, then ate her own with slow, skeptical defiance. Urgayle sampled the coffee and pronounced it “less hazardous than expected.”

As the meeting broke, Xander caught O’Neil at the door.

“You really don’t care what we do as long as nobody makes the news?”

“Not true,” she said. “We care. We just recognize you’re the only ones with a clue what you’re doing. If we take that away, we lose.”

“That’s not what I expected.”

“You’ll get used to it,” O’Neil said.

Xander doubted it, but for now, the system held.

In the rented office, Chegwidden watched the monitors and saw nothing amiss. He made a note: “First contact: operational. Trust: baseline. Friction: high, but contained.”

He almost smiled.

The world was not ready for Sunnydale.

But it was now, at least, on the clock.

Scene 2

At 11:12 a.m., Joyce Summers was stapling shipping manifests at the gallery office when her new cell phone erupted with a sound like a broken smoke alarm. She had assigned the “federal line” ringtone to something subtle, but the configuration had reset during the last software update. The gallery intern dropped an entire stack of acrylics. Joyce thumbed the green icon and tried to keep her voice level.

“Summers.”

A voice she didn’t know—female, precise, practiced at customer-service vowels—announced: “This is an operational readiness test. Please state the emergency protocol password and verify the secure code.”

Joyce’s mind blanked for half a second. She reached for the envelope she’d placed at the bottom of her bag, only to realize it was still on the kitchen counter where she’d last reviewed it. The intern attempted to restack the acrylics but succeeded only in turning a minor mess into a field of plastic dominoes.

“I, uh—could you hold a moment?” Joyce clamped the phone between shoulder and ear, spread the gallery files in front of her, and scanned for the half-remembered protocol printout. “This is a drill,” she repeated to herself. Not an attack. Not a real call. The context helped, but only slightly.

She found the packet, fumbled the tab open, and read off the code phrase. The woman on the other end repeated it back, asked her to verify the numbers at the bottom of page two, and then explained that, in the event of an “active kinetic event,” Joyce would be required to shelter in place, activate the local comms kit, and initiate a call tree which included at least three other named parties. Only one of whom she actually recognized: Giles.

Joyce wrote the instructions on a sticky note as the protocol officer read them off. Her handwriting, normally a point of pride, was almost illegible, and her fingers left a faint smudge where she clutched the pen too tightly.

“Do you understand the protocol, ma’am?”

“Yes,” Joyce lied. “I—yes, I do.”

“Would you like to run through a dry test?”

She hesitated. The intern had finally rebuilt the acrylic tower, and three other gallery staffers were now peering in from the hallway. It was a Tuesday, which meant the next hour was unlikely to require her expertise. Or so she hoped.

“Yes,” Joyce said, and as soon as she hung up she was dialing Giles.

He answered on the first ring. “Summers residence?”

“It’s me. They’re doing the drill.”

“Excellent,” Giles said. “It’s important to establish a baseline—”

“I can barely remember my own password. They want me to call two more people. Are you one of them?”

“I am. The second is likely Mrs. Rosenberg, and the third may be a liaison at the Navy office.”

Joyce exhaled. “Shouldn’t you be doing this, Giles?”

There was a pause. “They’ve determined, not without reason, that redundancy is preferable to sole reliance on an individual currently under observation for, as the admiral put it, ‘chronic overwork and probable sleep deficit.’”

Joyce closed her eyes. “That would be you.”

“I can confirm,” Giles said dryly.

She called Willow’s mother, got voicemail, left the required message, and then dialed the Sunnydale Navy number. The woman at the other end responded with the same sequence, as if this were the fourth time she’d done it today. Which, Joyce realized, it probably was.

By the time she hung up, the staffers had drifted away and the intern was pretending to sweep the rug with a two-inch paintbrush.

Joyce sat down. Her hand trembled a little. The phone vibrated again—this time, a text from the Navy: DRILL COMPLETE. RESPONSE: TIMELY. NO ACTION REQUIRED. She stared at the message, then scrolled up to see the last real conversation with Buffy, from the night before. The juxtaposition nearly undid her.

In a windowless office, Chegwidden reviewed the drill report.

“She hesitated, but completed the tree,” O’Neil said.

“She’s never had a protocol,” Chegwidden replied. “That was the point.”

Urgayle said, “Stress response: nonzero, but within parameters.”

“Repeat in three weeks,” Chegwidden said. “If she fails, we repeat in one. If she succeeds, we move to actual event simulation.”

O’Neil watched the data come in. “She’s worried she’s the weak link.”

Chegwidden drummed a finger on the desk. “That’s the strength of it. She knows the stakes. The ones who don’t worry—those are the ones I want off my list.”

Urgayle said, “Summers family response has improved in every metric.”

Chegwidden made a note. “Keep it that way.”

Back at the gallery, Joyce refilled her mug, wrote “ACTIVATE CALL TREE” on a fresh post-it, and stuck it to the phone case. She wasn’t sure if the exercise had made her more ready, or just more anxious. But she had not failed, and nobody had called to replace her with a specialist or a consultant or, God forbid, a professional parent.

For the rest of the day, every time the phone buzzed, she felt ready enough to answer.

Scene 3

“Field day,” O’Neil called it, and none of them laughed until Xander nearly killed himself trying to demonstrate a proper lifting technique on the park’s commemorative boulder. He wore civilian clothes, but his sneakers were one size too large—on Chegwidden’s recommendation, in case he needed to shed them in an emergency. Urgayle supervised the perimeter as if expecting a sniper in every family sedan. The comms specialist, who had not been introduced by name, spent the whole time staring at the sky and scribbling on an unmarked legal pad.

Xander took the support element out the southern edge of town, where the pavement thinned and the valley dropped into chaparral and dry riverbed. Sunnydale was visible behind them as a haze of palm trees, cheap tile roofs, and electrical substations.

“This is the fun part,” Xander said, gesturing with both arms. “No traffic, no cameras, and nobody to see you fly except the odd, very disappointed hawk.”

O’Neil checked her phone. “We have fifteen minutes before the check-in call.”

“I’ll have you back in ten. Twenty if you want to see something cool.”

Urgayle scanned the horizon. “Show us.”

Xander jogged to the old county line, now a battered road sign almost invisible behind yucca and sage. The comms guy timed the run with a stopwatch, then made a note that included “unexpected lateral acceleration.” Xander reappeared a full thirty meters past the line, slowed, and jogged back with barely an extra breath.

O’Neil tilted her head. “That was…”

“About seventy miles per hour, give or take the wind,” Xander said. “The boulder thing is for comic effect. This is the real trick.”

He leaned against the sign, waiting for one of them to ask the question.

Urgayle said, “Can you sustain that speed into the city?”

“I’ve done it,” Xander said. “But I usually slow up before residential areas. Debris, pets, kids—don’t want to make the news.”

O’Neil drew a small circle in her notebook. “And your top speed?”

“Tested at… I don’t know, Mach 2? 3? But only in a straight line, and not for long. You get weird stuff at that speed. Air pressure, heat, and sometimes—”

Xander stopped. Something about the line had changed. He felt it through his toes, like the static from an ungrounded carpet, but it was rooted and thick and threaded with something he couldn’t name.

O’Neil looked up. “Problem?”

Xander raised his hand. “Weird thing, but did you notice the sign?”

She looked. “Sunnydale, population forty-two thousand. Why?”

“Read the jurisdiction tag.”

She squinted at the bottom of the metal: “Maintained by City of Sunnydale, County of—” It was scratched, but the city seal was visible. “What am I looking for?”

“The marker’s different. It’s…”

He reached out, but his hand hit an invisible wall. It did not bounce, and it did not tingle, but it absolutely refused to let him through. He tried again, this time with both hands, and pressed as if pushing on a stubborn bathroom door. Nothing.

Urgayle tried to walk past him, but the boundary did not exist for anyone else. The comms guy, curious, jogged straight through with a wave. Xander tried again, and again, until O’Neil said, “Do you want to show us or not?”

He put his shoulder to it, found the pressure doubled, tripled, then simply failed to translate into movement. Xander stepped back.

“Try going the other way,” O’Neil said.

He did, walking into the wild nothing for ten meters, then circled back. This time, when he tried to re-enter Sunnydale, the resistance was absolute.

Xander looked at the ground. No physical markers, no pentagrams, nothing. The city itself had decided, somewhere between the cheap sign and the first fire hydrant, that he was no longer welcome.

Urgayle moved up next to him and tried to drag Xander forward, hand on his arm. Urgayle went through, Xander’s hand stayed stuck.

O’Neil called Chegwidden.

“Boundary event,” she said. “Subject Harris cannot re-enter city limits. Supernatural or otherwise, effect is total. Request guidance.”

On the other end, Chegwidden had already received a security alert. He toggled the speaker. “Harris, do you read?”

“Yeah, I read,” Xander said. “This is… not great.”

“Report sensation,” Chegwidden said.

Xander tried to keep the panic out of his voice. “It’s like the sidewalk is glue, but only for me. I can see the other side, I just can’t get there. No pain, no resistance for anyone else.”

Chegwidden said, “Repeat attempt with civilian escort.”

O’Neil called back the comms specialist, who had been recording everything on a GoPro hidden in a cell phone case. The specialist took Xander’s wrist and tried to tow him forward. The skin on Xander’s hand stretched, then simply stopped, like it was glued to glass. Xander looked at O’Neil, then back at his stuck wrist.

“Try at speed,” Urgayle said.

“That’s—”

“Try it,” O’Neil said.

Xander backed up, squared his shoulders, and ran straight at the line. He hit the boundary at full stride and fell hard, bouncing off nothing, rolling onto his shoulder. The boundary did not so much as ripple.

He sat up, rubbing his arm.

O’Neil offered a hand. “Anything broken?”

“Not physically.”

She pulled him to his feet. “We’re in uncharted territory.”

“Literally,” Xander said.

O’Neil placed a call to Giles and Buffy. “We have a boundary anomaly. Xander is currently outside the city and cannot return. Recommend regroup at the office.”

Buffy answered, “Is he hurt?”

O’Neil glanced at Xander. “No, but he’s… annoyed.”

Buffy’s voice, through the phone: “Tell him we’ll figure it out.”

They returned to the Navy office, minus Xander, who stood outside the sign and stared back at Sunnydale with a look reserved for people denied re-entry to their own birthday party.

Buffy and Giles arrived five minutes later. Willow had class, but texted from the library.

Buffy asked, “What happened?”

O’Neil explained, then produced the GoPro footage. Giles examined the video frame-by-frame. “It doesn’t have the characteristics of a force field,” he said. “No light, no diffraction. He simply ceases to move forward.”

Buffy shook her head. “So magic?”

Giles shrugged, for the first time admitting he had no answer. “Possibly. But it is not one I recognize.”

Urgayle suggested, “We try a different identity.”

Buffy perked up. “If Xander is in costume, does it still block him?”

O’Neil called him. “Superman up and try re-entry.”

They watched from the window as Xander took off his jacket, produced the suit from the duffel bag, and changed. He was visible from two blocks away, blue and red in the morning sun.

He walked to the boundary and tried again.

It didn’t work.

Xander gestured, frustrated, then tried to fly over the line. The effect was instantaneous: he rebounded thirty feet into the scrub, landing unhurt but deeply humiliated.

He called O’Neil. “Still a no-go. I don’t even make it a foot.”

Buffy turned to Giles. “What if it’s about the name?”

“Explain,” Giles said.

She pulled out her phone, showing the text from Willow: “Rook’s identity working separated Xander from Superman. Maybe only one can enter at a time?”

Giles pondered. “So what would the city recognize?”

O’Neil dialed Chegwidden. “Sir, if the theory is correct, we may need a legal or civic identity to permit re-entry.”

Chegwidden said, “Does the town possess an official record of his other identity?”

Buffy snorted. “They have his school files, disciplinary reports, attendance. All of it.”

Giles added, “There is a copy at City Hall, Principal Snyder’s office, and possibly the county records office.”

Chegwidden: “Retrieve them. We’ll test with the most recent record. Perhaps if the identity is physically present, it may allow entry.”

Buffy and Giles left for the school. O’Neil and Urgayle supervised, but kept to the background.

In Snyder’s office, the school secretary gave them a look like she expected a search warrant and a team of news reporters to follow. Giles asked for Xander’s file, saying he needed to review an old incident for a disciplinary review. The secretary retrieved a thick folder, banded in red, and handed it over with a look that said “You only think you have the whole story.”

Buffy took a copy of Xander’s most recent ID card. She snapped a picture and texted it to O’Neil.

O’Neil forwarded it to Xander. “Try again, but carry your ID. Announce your name as you cross.”

Buffy’s text arrived: “Willow thinks you need to introduce yourself as Alexander Harris, not Superman.”

He groaned, but tried it.

Xander walked up to the line, held up the ID, and said, “Alexander Lavelle Harris, returning from authorized absence. Please let me in.”

He pressed forward.

It gave.

Not much, but enough for him to get one foot past the line before he felt the pressure again. He staggered, then, holding the card in front of him, walked the last ten meters in slow, deliberate steps.

O’Neil watched from the window as Xander crossed into city limits, then immediately collapsed on the grass, breathing hard.

He called the office.

“It worked,” he said. “But I feel like I got mugged by a DMV clerk.”

Buffy and Giles drove back and found Xander leaning against the bus stop, still in the suit, ID in one hand.

Giles said, “You’re you again?”

“For now,” Xander said. “But it’s not a great feeling. I think if I put the card down, I get locked out again.”

Buffy looked at O’Neil. “What does this mean?”

O’Neil said, “It means the opposition has figured out how to weaponize bureaucracy against superpowers.”

Buffy shuddered. “That’s actually more terrifying than vampires.”

Giles said, “Is there a workaround?”

O’Neil thought for a moment. “We could forge IDs, rewrite the town registry, or hack the boundary directly. But I suggest we treat this as a temporary state, test for vulnerabilities, and avoid sending Xander outside the city unless we have a backup plan.”

Buffy nodded.

Urgayle looked at Xander. “Can you fight with one hand?”

“I can try,” Xander said.

O’Neil updated Chegwidden: “Boundary now keyed to legal identity. Supernatural asset cannot re-enter city unless carrying official documentation. We’ll recommend in-person status checks before any external deployments.”

Chegwidden replied, “Put it in the next report. Also, prepare for the possibility of civilian unmasking.”

O’Neil signed off.

Buffy watched Xander fumble his way back toward the office, the suit stretched at the knees, the ID held like a passkey to the worst amusement park on earth.

“Never thought I’d see the day,” she said.

“Which part?” Giles asked.

“Where the city hates Superman more than vampires.”

Giles smiled, small and sad. “I suspect the city hates the idea of anyone being two things at once.”

Buffy watched Xander walk, and for the first time since Sunnydale’s latest disaster, she wondered what would happen if the line finally closed behind them.

On Wednesday, Joyce was in her car, halfway to the post office, when the emergency line pulsed twice and went dead. She turned down the radio, waited for the second notification, but none came. A block ahead, the traffic light flickered yellow, then blue, then the usual red. Joyce hesitated. Sunnydale traffic did not justify stop-and-go, not at this hour, and most mornings the town behaved as if physics were still a live debate.

The phone vibrated in her purse. She parked at the curb, ignored a muttered horn from the only other driver on the block, and checked the alert.

SECURITY NOTICE: SUSPICIOUS ACTIVITY NEAR YOUR LOCATION. REMAIN IN VEHICLE. AWAIT INSTRUCTIONS.

She took a breath, snapped the purse shut, and watched the sidewalk.

Thirty seconds later, a follow-up message arrived.

IF SAFE, CALL 415-XXX-XXXX. VERIFY CODE ALPHA-ONE-NINE BEFORE DISCUSSION.

Joyce did as instructed, only realizing afterward that her hands were slick on the phone. The number picked up on the second ring; the voice was O’Neil’s, clipped and unhurried.

“Summers?”

“Yes—this is Joyce.”

“Remain in your car. Keep the doors locked. We have a transient anomaly in the vicinity of the library.”

“Is it—”

“Containment only. No direct threat. However, a local entity was observed attempting to enter the library during open hours. We do not believe it is aware of you.”

Joyce processed this. “Are Buffy and Giles—”

“They are safe. Asset Harris is in place. This is a containment drill, but with real observation. You are the closest responder.”

Joyce’s voice threatened to crack. “I don’t know what I’m supposed to do.”

“Monitor your surroundings. Do not attempt to engage. If you observe anything unusual, describe it as directly as possible. If you see the entity, note direction and clothing. If not, wait for the all-clear.”

The instructions ended. Joyce waited, feeling the engine idle vibrate under her shoes. She made a list of possible “unusual” things: vampires, demons, men in suits, or perhaps nothing at all.

Four minutes passed before she saw the shape: a very thin man, pale as bleached paper, wearing a charity-run t-shirt, pushing a battered shopping cart loaded with electronics, two spiral notebooks, and what appeared to be a cello case.

She called in the description, feeling faintly ridiculous.

“Entity observed,” she said. “Eastbound, cart, possible violin or… cello case. No threat. Just walking.”

O’Neil’s voice: “Copy. Watch for any interaction with public, or approach to vehicles.”

The shape paused at the end of the block, then crossed the street in front of her car. Joyce ducked low, just for a second. The figure ignored her, adjusted the cart, and kept moving. Joyce let out a breath.

Within seconds, two men in city-employee jumpsuits emerged from a nearby maintenance shed. They trailed the entity at a safe distance. A civilian vehicle—definitely not a real cop, but possibly the Navy medic from the day before—idled into view.

Joyce updated the line. “Approaching Park Avenue. No contact with public. Looks… sad, honestly.”

“Observed. Maintain position.”

Five minutes later, O’Neil called back.

“Situation resolved. Stand down.”

Joyce released her seatbelt, set the phone down, and then realized she had not checked her mirrors the whole time.

She started the car. Her hands were still trembling.

At the Navy office, Urgayle entered a brief report.

CITIZEN RESPONSE: PROMPT. DESCRIPTIVE. NO ESCALATION. SUBJECT MAINTAINED VISUAL WITHOUT COMPROMISING POSITION.

Chegwidden reviewed it. “She handled it.”

O’Neil added, “She’ll feel like she didn’t.”

“That is better than the alternative,” Chegwidden said. “Put her on monthly drills. Increase to weekly if response time drops.”

Urgayle nodded. “Message discipline?”

“She tells the truth,” Chegwidden said.

“Even when she’s scared.”

He signed off.

At home that night, Joyce went through the after-action report with Buffy.

“I know it wasn’t a real attack,” Joyce said, “but I was useless. I just sat there and stared at the man.”

Buffy frowned. “If you’d gotten out, it would have gone wrong. The rules are to not get involved.”

Joyce touched her daughter’s hand. “I don’t want to mess up and get you hurt. Or anyone else.”

“You did everything right,” Buffy said, and for the first time, it sounded like she meant it.

The next day, Chegwidden mailed Joyce a thank-you card. It was unsigned, but it contained a post-it: “Perfect execution of protocol. You are an asset.”

Joyce put it on her fridge. Under the magnet with the yellow dog.

Scene 5

They planned the next crossing as a controlled test. O’Neil, Urgayle, and the comms specialist set up cameras and physiological sensors at the edge of town. Buffy and Willow brought backup, both magical and medical, and Giles arrived with two briefcases of books and three more crossbow bolts than he had hands for.

Xander paced the line. He felt it before he saw it—a pressure behind the eyes, a building charge in the molars, the urge to shiver despite the ninety-degree air. The boundary remembered him, and this time it was not merely bureaucratic. It was personal.

Buffy handed him the ID card. “You want to try it, or should we call it off?”

He held up the card. “If this doesn’t work, I might get stuck out there. Or in-between.”

She nodded. “It’s your call.”

“Yeah. That’s the problem.”

He approached the sign. There was no physical marker, but to his senses it burned like a weld. He steeled himself, then walked forward.

The first step was easy. The second felt like wading through honey.

On the third step, something inside Xander split—an internal seam, a doubled voice, a loud, relentless accounting of everything he was, and everything he was not.

He heard O’Neil’s voice over the headset. “Harris, you okay?”

Xander wanted to answer, but his mouth couldn’t pick a side. The words hung in his throat, fighting for purchase.

He took a fourth step, the world shearing like a mirror in an earthquake. He saw himself reflected in both directions: on one side, Superman, blue and rigid and defined by certainty; on the other, Alexander Harris, sixteen, average height, a kid who spent every night wishing for something impossible.

The boundary made a choice.

It tried to keep both, and in the process, tore Xander sideways.

He collapsed to his knees at the edge of the sidewalk, one hand gripping the ID so tight it cut his palm, the other stretched out behind him, fingers splayed and trembling. To everyone else, it looked like a faint shadow in the air—a ripple, then a flicker, and then a second Xander, blurry and indistinct, a step behind.

Buffy ran forward, but O’Neil held her back. “Wait. It’s not stable.”

Giles watched, jaw clenched. “The working is attempting to separate the two identities.”

Willow whispered, “Like a split personality. Only real.”

Buffy said, “We have to help him.”

O’Neil nodded. “Let it finish. Otherwise we don’t know what we’re rescuing.”

The two images of Xander strained against each other. The Superman side tried to stand, but the Harris side clung to the ground, resisting. The air snapped, once, and a pulse of ozone rolled across the lot.

Then the Xanders merged, momentarily, and the body at the line collapsed into one—trembling, alive, but breathing so shallowly that O’Neil signaled the medic.

“Get him clear. Move him to the shade.”

Buffy and Willow dragged Xander out of the direct sun. He was pale, cold, sweating. Giles knelt beside him.

“Harris? Can you hear me?”

Xander opened his eyes, and for a second they were blue, then brown, then blue again. He blinked, and the effect faded.

“Yeah,” he said. “I’m here. We’re here.”

He tried to sit, but the medic held him down and checked his pulse, then flashed a light in each eye.

Buffy asked, “Can you feel both?”

Xander nodded. “They’re not separate. But they’re not together either. It’s like having two radios, and both are always on.”

O’Neil said, “Is it manageable?”

Xander shrugged. “Ask me after I try to sleep.”

Urgayle helped him to his feet. “You functioned as two, then one. Is there a chance you could stay that way?”

Giles answered, “Given the strength of the working, it’s possible the separation is now a permanent risk. The intent was not just to bar entry, but to force a schism in identity.”

O’Neil said, “If it happens again, will you warn us?”

Xander smiled, small and grim. “Hard not to.”

The medic taped a gauze square over the cut in his palm and gave him water. Xander drank, then looked at the others.

Buffy squeezed his shoulder. “We’re not letting it split you. We’ll figure it out.”

He nodded, but the look in his eyes said it was already too late for figuring.

Back at the office, Chegwidden read the incident report, then wrote a new protocol in red ink: “If identity working is suspected, all crossings to be supervised and logged. Monitor for duplication, schism, and any evidence of parallel existence. Report immediately. Do not assume re-integration is benign.”

He closed the file.

Buffy waited with Xander in the shade, watching as he finished the bottle and tried to joke about failed science experiments. The support team packed up the gear. Willow started researching city records for a way to reverse the working, but Xander knew, deep down, that the effect was not designed for reversal.

He watched the sun move across the pavement, and wondered how many versions of himself would be left by morning.

Chapter 24: "Jurisdiction"

Scene 1

Xander’s watch vibrated in two sharp, precisely timed pulses. He didn’t look at it right away. He kept his eyes on the sidewalk, on the exact place where the town’s working had drawn its invisible, immovable line. It felt the way an oncoming front feels on the skin, pressure dropping out of nowhere, just enough to make you swallow for no reason. If he reached out, he could almost believe his hand would meet resistance—dense air or static or the thick skin of the world pulled over Sunnydale to keep him in, or keep something else out.

He waited for the third pulse. None came. The alert pattern was only for noncritical, civilian-assist traffic, the kind the Navy had gotten good at farming out as training missions for Xander Harris, Heroic Minor, but still too urgent to ignore. He thumbed the watch face, checked the icon. Nothing from the home team, no direct line from Giles or Willow, just the generic emergency overlay blinking quietly against the digital numbers.

On the street behind him, a woman jogged past with headphones, not even glancing at the kid in jeans, sneakers, and a military windbreaker he’d found at a thrift store. If he called out, she would look at him like he was invisible, the way everyone in town had started looking at him after the first three headlines. The way even his own mother looked at him when he brought groceries in, making sure to keep a “Xander did not rob a liquor store” joke on the tip of his tongue so she’d laugh and go back to pretending everything was fine.

He pressed the side button, short-short, as O'Neil had shown him. It sent a clean digital refusal: unable to respond, not declining future requests, just not now. The watch didn’t buzz to confirm. It went silent. On the sidewalk, nothing happened. Xander waited for the shame to arrive, but it didn’t—not at first. Not until he imagined the call routing through whatever disaster was happening, firefighters waiting, a crew short one pair of hands, and the inescapable knowledge that if he jogged over the town limit and put on the suit, he could be there in half an hour, hour tops, problem solved, nobody else even needed to know.

But crossing the line meant taking the risk: the split, the sidereal hangover, the days of feeling like a tourist in his own body while the rest of him drifted outside the boundary, refusing to merge.

The mission would survive. Someone else would make up the time, or the loss would be entered in the log as Acceptable, per standing order.

He took a step away from the boundary, feeling the pressure ease off the back of his neck. The watch face stayed blank.

*

At Coronado, the watch floor was in the first lull of the day. Two analysts traded passwords under their breath, and the comms specialist, a pale-skinned petty officer who looked like she’d been recruited directly from a hacker sleepaway camp, was juggling three secure lines and a high-caffeine beverage in a battered travel mug. Lieutenant O‘Neil stood by the whiteboard, logging the last four anomalies and circling two with a red marker.

The duty officer at the comms desk nodded once and said, “Asset Harris unavailable, signal received.”

O’Neil didn’t look up. “Reassign.”

“Next up is SG team two, Mountain contact.”

She made a note. “If the incident is still active, send over the observer logs. Give them the heads up on why we’re not deploying the usual.”

The petty officer replied with a precise “Aye, ma’am,” then returned to typing. The SG contact in Colorado took the briefing without comment, and the mission re-tasked itself in under thirty seconds. Nobody asked why the asset was out. Nobody speculated, gossiped, or even used a euphemism. The protocol was the protocol. If the world didn’t allow for assets who needed a day off, it wasn’t a world O’Neil intended to live in, much less enforce.

She underlined the incident in red, wrote “COMPLETE” next to it, and erased the action item from the board.

*

Wildfire containment in the Angeles National Forest burned through an extra forty minutes and two EMTs got dehydrated before the evening relief team came in. The Navy’s after-action report flagged the delay, but not the reason. The local hospital prepped for overrun and only had to divert a single case, a minor laceration, to a neighboring facility. Nobody was hurt. Nobody died. The system held.

*

At the boundary, Xander stood very still. The sidewalk was empty, the watch face dark, the town itself almost too quiet, as if waiting for him to dare it.

He let his jaw unclench. One degree. Two.

He looked at the line, then looked away.

He walked home, and for the first time in weeks, he didn’t check the watch again until he was through his own front door.

Scene 2

Chegwidden’s exit left a vacuum in the kitchen, which Joyce attempted to fill with the scent of coffee she didn’t intend to drink. She rinsed the carafe twice, ground the beans by hand, and measured each spoonful like an oath, as if a perfectly calibrated cup might keep the next two hours on course.

Buffy perched on one side of the table, hands folded on the laminate, her eyes already skimming the draft protocol. She read in hard, vertical lines, pausing only to circle a clause or underline a word that failed to meet her personal standard of bullshit detection. O’Neil sat across from her, posture military but hands relaxed. Urgayle had positioned herself in the far corner, back to the wall and a notebook open, but her eyes drifted from the page to the windows, to the exits, to every break in the perimeter.

Joyce poured the first mug and set it near Buffy, careful not to spill, then hovered with a second until O’Neil said, “Thank you, ma’am, but I’m on clock.” Urgayle shook her head once, declining by absence rather than voice.

After the third pass with the mug, Joyce retreated, carrying her own upstairs. She paused at the landing, listened to the absence of voices, and let the adults handle themselves.

Buffy’s first salvo came before O’Neil finished opening her own copy. “Page two, hostile entity definition. Your language says ‘any non-human entity demonstrating aggressive behavior toward civilians.’” She tapped her finger on the margin. “That covers about three-quarters of the town, and all of the lunch crowd at Hot Dog on a Stick. It needs to be narrower.”

O’Neil tilted her head, the way you do when you know the other side has a point but you want to make them say it. “Operationally, we’re directed to intervene before civilian harm. That’s not a lot of room.”

Buffy didn’t blink. “Then the designation is mine. If I say it’s not a threat, your people stand down. If I say it is, you have my explicit authorization. But you don’t get to shoot first and explain later just because the target has scales or an unfortunate dental plan.”

Urgayle marked a check in his notebook.

O’Neil considered. “Add: ‘Active threat as determined by local lead or designated field commander.’ That gives you the call.”

Buffy grinned, but not like she was happy. “Field commander is me, unless I’m dead or stuck in a boundary working.”

O’Neil wrote it in, longhand. “Noted.”

The next section was all infrastructure: medical, transport, chain of custody on anything weird enough that the coroner or the EPA would run screaming. Buffy skimmed, made a show of not caring, but signed off on the part where every patrol wound meant a Navy medic on call, and all incident reporting would be under an alias unless she opted in.

Urgayle pointed with her pen: “You left out the ninety-second rule.”

Buffy frowned. “What’s that?”

“If any of our people enter your designated patrol zone without prior clearance, they check in every ninety seconds. No more shadowing you, no more unannounced backup. If we miss a check-in, we fall back, and you get the call.”

Buffy glanced at O’Neil, then at Urgayle. “You’ve done this before.”

Urgayle shrugged, an understated motion. “Never with a minor in charge. But more often than anyone wants to write down.”

Buffy marked a plus sign in the margin.

Third fight: jurisdiction.

Buffy said, “If a supernatural incident overlaps with a civilian crime scene, your people do not talk to Sunnydale PD.”

O’Neil waited for elaboration.

Buffy gave it. “Not because I don’t trust you. Because I don’t trust them. They’re not equipped. Half the time they’re in the pocket of the Mayor, and the other half, they’re in the pocket of whoever last bought them a round at Willy’s. You keep your support crew in the background, and I’ll handle Snyder and the rest.”

O’Neil nodded, no argument, just a dry, “Confirmed.”

The rest was routine, as far as multi-jurisdictional supernatural operations were ever routine: “Medical extraction for you and your associates after every engagement, no questions asked. Equipment resupply through a dead-drop at the storage unit on Maple. Weekly intelligence summary of any signals traffic that mentions Sunnydale, even if it’s just tabloid stuff or satellite noise. Standing offer of forensic analysis for any artifact Giles can’t identify with his own team.”

Buffy, surprised, said, “That’s actually useful.”

O’Neil made no show of pride. “If the goal is support, it has to be support.”

They reviewed the final clause together: “If I want the base to leave, the base leaves.”

Buffy asked, “Has anyone ever taken you up on that?”

O’Neil paused. “Once. It ended poorly, but not because we stayed out.”

Buffy didn’t ask for details. She flipped to the signature line, considered, then asked, “What happens if I change my mind?”

“You call. We meet again.”

Buffy took the pen from the counter, the red one, and signed her name in three quick strokes. “If you violate, I get to rescind unilaterally.”

“That’s the contract,” O’Neil said.

Urgayle countersigned for the Navy, folded the copy in half, and tucked it into her pocket. “We’ll be at the storage unit Thursday at six. If you need it sooner, call.”

Buffy said, “Will you bring donuts, or is that the support unit’s job?”

Urgayle, almost smiling, said, “We’ll bring something better.”

The meeting ended without a handshake. O’Neil stood, smoothed her uniform, and said, “If it helps, I never got a protocol signed on the first try. Not even in D.C.”

Buffy said, “That’s because you never did it with a teenager who grew up on custody battles and TV law.”

O’Neil gave her a look of genuine respect. “See you Thursday, Buffy.”

They let themselves out.

Buffy sat back, exhaled, and stared at her name in red.

Joyce, waiting at the top of the stairs, didn’t ask how it went. She brought down an extra mug and made sure it was warm.

Scene 3

Xander perched on the corner of the Sunnydale High roof, knees pulled tight and heels braced on the corrugated lip of the HVAC housing. Below him, three stories of unremarkable brick dropped to a courtyard overgrown with half-dead lawn and the faded paint lines of a running track nobody had bothered to resurface since the Reagan administration. His satchel rested on the steel beside him, the Superman suit folded inside a textbook sleeve labeled WORLD HISTORY: MODERN.

He keyed the secure line, watched the LED blink green, and waited for the connection.

Zoey picked up on the second ring, already mid-thought. “—which is ridiculous because if you can’t trust a Georgetown ethics seminar to go off the rails, what can you trust, right? Tell me you have something better.”

He smiled. “You have no idea how much better. I’m on a high school roof in California, and I just watched a squirrel walk off with someone’s Cheetos. Like, the whole bag. One mouthful.”

“Sunnydale wildlife is advanced,” Zoey said. “Is this the good line?”

“Direct to the secure,” Xander said. “No party line, no Mrs. Landingham.”

A pause, then, “Is she still calling you Superman?”

“Not in public. But I’m pretty sure she calls me the ‘asset’ when she thinks I can’t hear. It’s dehumanizing, but in an endearing, pastry-based way.”

Zoey laughed, a real laugh, and the tension of the last two days unwound in the space between them. “So what’s up? Is this a just-because call or an imminent-disaster call?”

He leaned back and watched a cloud slide over the sun. “A little of both. I got an alert this morning, boundary-side. Something in the Angeles. I didn’t go.”

He waited for the punchline, or the letdown, or the disappointment. Zoey just said, “Good.”

He blinked. “Not even going to ask why?”

“Don’t need to. You told me if you ever did it, you’d be the last to believe it. So, I believe you.”

Xander found himself gripping the edge of the roof. “Any fallout on your end?”

Zoey was quiet for a moment. “I just… I saw the news. Angeles National Forest, the wildfire. They said nobody died, but the containment took longer than expected. I wondered if that was the thing you didn’t go to.”

“That was it. Me not showing up.” He closed his eyes.

“Then I’m glad you didn’t,” she said, and her voice had that particular steadiness that meant she was choosing her words carefully. “The person you answer to—the woman who trained you. Is she okay with it?”

He shook his head, realized she couldn’t see him, and said, “She’s not the type. She’d just turn it into a training module.”

Zoey laughed, soft. “She sounds terrifying.”

“She’s actually kind of great. In a ‘will make you run until you vomit’ way.”

“All the best people are.”

They sat in companionable silence, distant but closer than anyone else in their respective radius. Xander looked out at the town. “You ever get the feeling that it’s all one big boundary working? Like, not just the magic, but the PTA meetings, and the mall, and every day you go home and your parents have the same argument about whose turn it is to forget to buy milk?”

Zoey said, “That’s every campus in the world, Xander. Only difference is you have actual vampires. We just have hedge fund recruiters.”

“I’ll take vampires. At least they’re honest about their nutritional needs.”

She snorted. “Are you okay? Not superhero-okay, but real-people-okay.”

He closed his eyes and weighed the answer. “If I wasn’t, I wouldn’t have called.”

“That is the least comforting answer and also the most honest.”

He smiled again, less forced. “How’s your end of things?”

Zoey described a minor Secret Service shuffling, a professor who hated her father on principle, and the elaborate choreography required to eat a sandwich in the Georgetown food court without being photographed. She finished by saying, “If you ever want to disappear, I can teach you tricks. Nobody notices a girl with her face in a book and a Starbucks cup welded to her hand.”

Xander said, “If I ever cross the boundary for good, I’ll take you up on it.”

She was quiet for a long moment, then: “They still haven’t figured out what you are, have they?”

He answered, “I’m pretty sure that’s the whole problem.”

Somewhere below, a door slammed open. Xander heard the flat thud of footsteps on the stairwell, then the slow squeal of the roof access door. He turned, expecting a vagrant or a patrol, and instead found one of the school security guards frozen in the doorway, looking at him with a mixture of horror and resignation.

“Hi,” Xander said, friendly.

The guard blinked. “You… you good?”

“Perfectly safe. Just making a call.”

“Uh, don’t… fall off?”

“Wouldn’t dream of it.”

The guard closed the door and retreated. Xander heard the footsteps echo away.

“Still there?” Zoey asked.

“Always,” he said.

“Gotta run. Ethics is back on the rails.”

He wanted to say something—something heroic, or comforting, or even normal—but Zoey beat him to it. “Don’t change the world tonight. Just keep the line open.”

He clicked off, and the sky seemed one shade lighter.

Scene 4

The library’s after-hours quiet was the kind of silence that didn’t just invite conspiracy, it demanded it. The main reading table had been converted into a war room: Giles’s annotated map of the town’s ley-line grid sprawled across one half, Willows’s laptop open to a recursive loop of highlighted intersections, and three mugs—one tea, two black coffee—circling like satellites around a central spill of yellow legal paper.

Ethan, who had acquired a bright white dress shirt since his last visit, was on the floor drawing circles in chalk with the careful, practiced flick of a man who’d done this in far dirtier places. Each stroke landed at the precise tangent, forming interlocking arcs that looked almost decorative until you noticed the sigils embedded at three points in the perimeter.

Buffy leaned against the magazine rack, one foot propped on the lowest shelf, chewing an apple with the slow deliberation of a person who knew the value of vitamin C but had other things on her mind. She watched the team, eyes tracking from Ethan’s chalk work to Willow’s code window to Giles’s muttered recitations from the Survey map.

Giles, peering through his glasses at a section marked “Old Town—Possible Node,” said, “The logic is sound. If we collapse the secondary anchor, the primary should be left exposed, if not weakened.”

Willow ran her script and made a sound halfway between triumph and exasperation. “We’ve got the candidate location. Cross-referenced with the city’s public works database and Ethan’s sigil log. It’s at the corner of Maple and Crestwood—right under a defunct pay phone.”

Ethan completed the outer ring and stood, dusting off his hands. “It’s never the primary anchor. That’s for show. The ones that matter are always under garbage infrastructure or public utilities. If I ever take over a town, I’m putting my soul jar in a Taco Bell drive-thru.”

Buffy said, “That’s a lot of effort to keep people away from free long distance.”

Willow angled her laptop to show the satellite image. “The shell company that pulled the original permit is owned by a Wolfram & Hart sub-subsidiary. Giles recognized the accountant’s name.”

Giles looked over his glasses. “Of course I did. The firm handles estate closure for the Council. They’re the worst kind of efficient.”

Buffy crunched her apple and asked, “What’s the plan?”

Ethan pointed at her, then himself. “We go. You break cover if there’s trouble, I handle the counter-seal. Giles and Willow coordinate from here. Willow patches us into the police and EMS, Giles runs interference if we trip an alarm.”

Buffy tossed the apple core into the bin. “If?”

He shrugged. “We’re being optimistic tonight.”

In five minutes they were out the door, Buffy in black hoodie and jeans with the patched elbow, Ethan carrying a nylon bag of tools and a flashlight with the lens covered by a square of red film. Giles handed Buffy a walkie-talkie and said, “Channel four. Willow has the other end.”

Maple and Crestwood was a three-block walk and a universe away from the school’s nervous lighting. The defunct pay phone was tucked into a low brick wall with a badly painted blue metal shell, the receiver missing and the coin slot covered with a square of duct tape. Buffy scoped the street, saw nothing but a single parked car, and gave Ethan the nod.

He set to work on the concrete base, chiseling away a line of mortar where the footing met the sidewalk. Buffy watched the end of the street, counting seconds and listening for anything out of pattern: a car alarm, a cough, the clang of a trash can lid.

Ethan pried loose a flat obsidian disc the size of a half-dollar. “There’s our anchor.”

Buffy said, “If you drop it, do we get seven years of bad luck or just a lot of paperwork?”

“Depends who’s holding the paperwork.” He produced a small cold iron chisel from his bag, positioned it over a notched indentation, and whispered a phrase that made Buffy’s ears ring, just slightly. He struck once, hard, and the disc split in two with a high, brittle sound.

A faint whiff of ozone curled up, and the streetlight overhead blinked out for three seconds before returning to normal.

They pocketed the pieces, covered the scar with the pay phone shell, and retraced their steps to the library, where Willow waited in the entry with the door propped open and the laptop clutched to her chest.

“Did it work?” she asked.

“Nothing exploded, so probably,” Buffy said.

Ethan placed the two halves of the obsidian disc on the table. “We’ll know if the boundary weakens at all. That was a textbook anchor, but well-hidden.”

Giles, pulling at the corners of his map, said, “Any sense of pushback?”

Buffy shook her head. “Quieter than most patrols. No sign of anyone watching.”

Ethan, staring at the broken disc, frowned. He turned both pieces over and studied the inner faces, then went very still.

“What?” Willow said, instantly anxious.

Ethan set the pieces side by side, aligning the break. The inner faces of the disc were covered with a precise grid of hashmarks and tiny engraved numbers, like the dial of a scientific instrument. He traced a finger along one quadrant and said, “This wasn’t a structural anchor. It was a gauge.”

Buffy leaned in. “As in… measuring something?”

“Measuring who,” Ethan said. “This would record the force and the nature of the breach. If you break it, it logs the energy signature—magical, kinetic, physical, whatever—and the direction. Probably the time, too.”

Willow peered at the engravings. “So… it’s a tripwire. Someone’s going to know exactly how we did it.”

Giles picked up the disc, examined it with a small jeweler’s lens he kept in his vest, and muttered, “Ingenious. A direct logging device. They’ll have a complete profile of the energy you used, and, if I’m not mistaken, the identity of the practitioner.”

Buffy stiffened. “So we just handed over all our stats to the enemy.”
