Daniel angled the mug to avoid splashing her workstation and squinted. “That’s… that’s not a typical resonance. It’s got a sort of—what’s the word?—crystal habit? No, wait, that’s geology. But this is… mechanical. Or engineered.”

She pulled up the raw return, cycling the color gradient from cold blue to a livid orange just for effect. “It’s four kilometers down, but it’s throwing off a signal like a tuning fork. The energy budget doesn’t make sense unless something is feeding it, or the structure is huge.”

Daniel’s eyes darted between the readout and his own memory palace. “Subglacial lakes are possible, but there’d be a thermal signature, and the lateral spread would be more diffuse. This is almost… planar? Like a plate?”

She already had the overlay queued. “I ran it against the last two weeks of seismic. The node is static. No migration, no fade. It’s either been inert for a very long time, or it just woke up.”

He set the mug down on the console, almost reverently. “That’s not good.”

Carter allowed herself a thin smile. “I’m not betting on ‘good’ until we get eyes on it.”

The third member of the ops ring, standing just inside Carter’s peripheral vision, was Teal’c. He wore the usual SGC-issue wool and stood with his arms folded, jaw set in a way that suggested he’d been watching both the screen and their conversation for several minutes. Teal’c did not glance away from the display.

On the third monitor, she’d left up a topographic render of the anomaly, a wireframe cross-section. Teal’c stepped forward, not breaking stride, and leaned in to tap the upper right corner of the shape. The gesture was precise; the kind of move that said he knew exactly what he wanted to see and didn’t need to ask.

Carter zoomed in. The corner came into focus, the lines resolving into an angular geometry that was all right angles and hard planes, nothing like the slouched, rounded forms of buried rock.

Teal’c’s voice was quiet but authoritative. “This structure is not natural.”

Daniel made a noise somewhere between a cough and a laugh. “That’s what we were just—”

Teal’c: “It is not of this world, either.”

Daniel frowned, then tried to correct himself. “Not human, you mean.”

“I mean,” said Teal’c, “it is not of this world.”

Carter hesitated, then ran a comparison against the offworld artifact database—Daniel’s favorite toy, which she kept as a read-only drive to avoid accidentally tripping an international incident. The match algorithm hummed for a few seconds before returning “no close analog,” which was both expected and deeply unsettling.

Daniel shook his head. “If it’s not Starfleet, and it’s not the ship, and it’s not geological—”

Teal’c didn’t blink. “There are worlds where such structures are common. But the design is not Goa’uld. Not Ancient. It is… familiar, but not the same.”

Carter watched him for a second, then said, “Do you recognize something?”

Teal’c looked at the screen again, his focus total. “I am uncertain. I require time.”

She nodded, and began packaging the data for escalation. SGC protocol required a double-signoff for anomalous finds, and Hammond wasn’t due in for another hour, but she could mark the file for immediate review. She dumped the sensor suite’s full packet, flagged it with PRIORITY—ANOMALOUS SUBSURFACE STRUCTURE, and printed the report to the secure line.

She picked up the phone, dialed the direct to Hammond’s office, and, when it rolled to voicemail, switched lines to the White House situation room. “This is Major Carter, SGC operations. I have a Priority escalation, flagged for Doctor Weir and immediate circle.”

The voice on the other end recognized her, and within seconds she had a confirmation: Weir was on the schedule, open slot in seventeen minutes. Carter finished prepping the file, snapped the backup drive from the console, and handed it to Daniel, who took it without spilling his coffee.

Teal’c stepped back, eyes still locked on the now-magnified angular corner. “Major Carter,” he said, quietly. “If the structure is what I suspect, we must be prepared for… unexpected behavior.”

Carter glanced up. “Define unexpected.”

But Teal’c only shook his head, lips pressed in a line, and said, “It is best not to define until we are certain.”

Daniel tried to lighten the air, as usual. “Maybe it’s a good surprise.”

Carter gave him a look, then grinned. “On this program, ‘good surprise’ is an oxymoron.”

She watched as the printer spit out the final page, the anomaly’s cross-section etched in false color, its corners too sharp for comfort.

She squared the report, clipped it tight, and headed for the briefing room.

In her wake, the ops floor hummed on, business as usual, but the sense of something new—something not in any protocol—hung in the air like the first static charge before a lightning storm.

Scene 2

There was a particular climate to the White House Situation Room at quarter-past seven: the predawn gray seeping through the frosted windows, the aftertaste of strong coffee and diplomatic anxiety, and the faint, tart ozone of freshly unsealed classified envelopes. Weir stood at the head of the briefing table, her copy of the SGC sensor packet splayed in front of her, the logo on the top sheet still wet enough to transfer to her palm if she pressed down.

President Bartlet sat directly across from her, his tie already loosened, the deep furrow between his brows a familiar sight to anyone who’d spent a year in the West Wing. To his right, Leo McGarry was in reading-glasses mode, skimming the summary sheet with the thumb-and-forefinger intensity of a man who could recite the Antarctic Treaty verbatim but refused to trust anyone else’s translation. Josh Lyman anchored the end, two legal pads to his left and a government-issue pen uncapped and ready, his gaze flicking between the paperwork and Weir’s face as if searching for the flaw in a closing argument.

Weir led with the facts. “This packet contains the most recent sensor data from the recovery site at McMurdo. The anomaly is located approximately 4.1 kilometers beneath the Ross Ice Shelf, just east of the main crash furrow. It is a geometrically regular mass, angular, with a resonance profile inconsistent with either natural formation or any known terrestrial alloy. It predates the Enterprise’s arrival by several thousand years, according to sediment displacement and stratigraphy. The signal is persistent and does not match anything in the SGC or NORAD libraries.”

She paused to let them digest. Bartlet raised a hand, the one with the presidential signet, and rotated it in a small circle. “Doctor Weir, is there any indication that this… artifact, or whatever it is, has anything to do with our new neighbor?”

Weir did not blink. “We don’t know, sir. It is outside the hull perimeter. There is no clear connection, except for proximity and the fact that the signal only spiked after the Enterprise’s arrival.”

Leo asked, “Is there any evidence the ship’s crash activated it? Triggered something that wasn’t active before?”

Weir considered. “It’s possible, but the timeline is ambiguous. We have only three months of solid sensor coverage prior to the event, and the anomaly’s activity may have been below our detection threshold. The most significant change is the pattern: it is now repeating, almost like a coded handshake. That started two days after the saucer section stabilized.”

Josh’s pen did a little jig on the pad. “And we’re sure the Russians or the Chinese don’t have a remote drone under there, lighting this up just to see what we’ll do?”

Weir said, “We’re sure, unless someone’s figured out a way to tunnel four kilometers through Antarctic bedrock without leaving any evidence on the seismic net. The anomaly’s mass is at least thirty tons, possibly much more, and it’s cold enough to suggest it’s been inert for millennia.”

Josh grunted. “So we have a pre-existing alien… something, sitting right under the most sensitive site on Earth, and we only found out because a different spaceship crash-landed on top of it.”

“Yes,” said Weir.

Bartlet looked up, eyes sharp. “I assume the real issue is what happens when we try to dig it out.”

Weir nodded. “Legally, sir, we’re in a box. The Antarctic Treaty’s Environmental Protocol requires a separate excavation framework for anything not covered by the current salvage operation. If we try to lump this in with the Enterprise recovery, the Chinese and Russians will protest. They have observer teams in Christchurch already, and the PRC has stated—in writing—they expect to be involved in any new discoveries.”

Leo closed the folder and tapped the edge. “So we either get ahead of this and file for a separate mandate, or we risk a diplomatic incident.”

“Correct,” Weir said. “If we move now, we can set the framework ourselves. If we wait, we cede the process to the Treaty parties. Either way, it’s going to be a race.”

Josh asked, “How long to draft a charter?”

Weir said, “If I have full support from the Legal team, six hours. If I need to negotiate language with the State Department, twelve. The Chinese will file an injunction within twenty-four, so the window is tight.”

Leo looked to Bartlet. Bartlet leaned back and, for a moment, seemed to weigh the merits of inertia versus acceleration. Then he tapped a finger on the folder, a crisp snap. “Draft the excavation charter. Attach a science-first preamble—make it clear we’re not sending a tank division, we’re sending geologists and archaeologists. Include observer language that’s generous, but doesn’t give up our point of control.”

Josh asked, “And if we get a protest?”

“We’ll get a protest,” Bartlet said. “But we’ll have set the terms. I’d rather go to the mat with the Treaty parties than wait and have this turn into an open auction.”

Weir closed her copy of the sensor packet. “I’ll have the framework on your desk by end of day.”

Bartlet nodded, then said, “And, Elizabeth? If it turns out this thing is the front door to a Stargate or a wormhole or a second spaceship, I want your word that you’ll tell me before you tell the press.”

Weir’s eyes glinted. “Sir, if it’s a front door, I will tell you before I even turn the knob.”

Leo stifled a smile. Josh said, “That’s a hell of a quote for the briefing book.”

Weir already had her packet in hand, the notes she’d made in the margin now highlighted in yellow. She didn’t wait for the formal dismissal; she nodded once, and was out the door before the silence settled.

In the room, Bartlet watched the closed door for a long beat.

“Leo, how many people do you trust to dig up an alien artifact without breaking the world in half?”

“Today? Just her,” said Leo.

Josh twirled the pen, looked at his pad, and said, “I bet the Chinese have their own Weir.”

Leo: “I hope she’s half as competent.”

Bartlet sat back, gaze drifting to the anomaly’s angular corner, now etched in a glossy printout in front of him.

“Some days,” he said, “I wish the future would just take a number and wait its turn.”

Nobody argued with that.

Scene 3

Scene 4

The White House did not wait for the drill to reach the buried structure. Before the first rig turned, State opened the Antarctic Treaty channel and put the proposed excavation framework in front of every party with a standing claim or observer right.

China and Russia asked whether the discovery was connected to the Enterprise recovery. Australia and France asked for the scope of the work and the safety exclusions. The United Kingdom wanted a technical representative on the next call. Nobody was being asked to approve an American fait accompli; everyone was being given notice before the ground moved.

Bartlet’s instruction was simple: disclose the limited drilling plan, offer observer access, reserve operational control for the site team, and make clear that the formal mandate was still being negotiated. The legal language went out with the science packet, not after the first alarm made delay impossible.

By the time Weir finished the last call, the consultations were underway. None of the Treaty parties had surrendered a claim, but none could credibly say they had been excluded. Carter’s rigs had a narrow window, and the diplomacy had started before the machinery did.

The wind in Antarctica didn’t just howl; it rewrote the rules of pressure, trading decibels for pure, molecular spite. Even through the triple-insulated shell of the staging tent, Carter could feel the flex of the gusts as she keyed in the diagnostic for the third time, fingers so cold she doubted she’d ever get the sensation back. The console bolted to the scaffolding railing had a backup heater, but even at maximum it just kept the frost from forming on the screen. The ambient read -31 Celsius and falling.

They’d set up the four-rig array in less than twelve hours. The engineering crew—half SGC, half civilian, all certified maniacs—had worked through three shifts without sleep, their work punctuated by the staccato of mallets chipping ice from hydraulic lines and the occasional bark of a tech as another cable went brittle and snapped under the wind load. The rigs stood in a staggered line, each one perched on a tier of bolted-together scaffolding that could, in theory, support a tank battalion. At the center: Carter and her portable, the nerve center for the whole show.

O’Neill had claimed a spot behind her, arms crossed, balaclava drawn so tight only his eyes showed. He checked his watch every few minutes, then said things like, “You still in there, Carter?” or “Let me know if I need to send in a search and rescue.” He’d made it clear to the team that if anyone had to go on a rescue, they’d better bring snacks.

The drilling had been clean—almost too clean, if Carter was being honest. Each rig advanced at exactly the projected rate, the pressure on the bit never rising above green, the thermal drift so steady she began to suspect the computer was padding the data. At 818 meters, she saw the first anomaly: a tiny, repeating spike in the accelerometer, interval fourteen meters, each one sharper than the last. Not the signature of sediment; not the work of glacial scouring, which should have been a messy, low-pass filter of vibrations. This was deliberate. Engineered.

She flagged the event and paged Daniel, who’d been pacing the perimeter with a thermal mug and what looked like a third-hand notebook. He appeared instantly at her elbow.

“Let me see,” he said, breath frosting in the air.

She slid the screen so he could read. “Every fourteen meters, a spike. Dead regular.”

Daniel squinted. “Layered composite, maybe? That’s… it doesn’t fit the model.”

Carter pulled up the seismic overlay. “The echo shows lateral continuity. It’s not a vein or a fossil bed. It’s—”

“Stratified,” Daniel said. “As in, someone put it there on purpose.”

From behind, O’Neill: “I hope you’re talking about a thousand-year-old beer cellar. Because if it’s another Stargate, I’m not cleaning up the paperwork.”

Carter half-smiled but kept her focus on the numbers. At 871 meters, the resonance doubled in amplitude. The core sample from Rig One was dead ice, but the camera on Rig Three’s probe showed a faint gray line on the down-angle. She logged it, checked the depth, and called to the tech at Rig Three: “Can you focus the camera at 882 meters? There’s a discontinuity in the substrate.”

The tech—wrapped in three layers and cursing in a dialect Carter didn’t recognize—tapped his comm. “Copy, Major. Rotating to south azimuth.”

The image swung around, the lens rim fogging at the edges. The seam was obvious: a dark, almost metallic plane, parallel to the ice but too smooth to be natural. The bit chewed through the last six centimeters and stopped, as if it had hit air.

“Pressure’s dropping,” the tech said, voice suddenly sharp.

Carter checked the gauge. “Negative flow. We’re venting into a cavity.”

O’Neill uncrossed his arms. “You said ‘cavity.’ I don’t like cavities. Cavities mean something is waiting inside.”

Daniel said, “There’s no biological signature. No methane spike. If there’s life, it’s not like anything we’ve seen before.”

Carter: “Or it’s dormant.”

The tech toggled the camera light. A halogen pulse lit up the void, and for a split second the feed went white with snow, then resolved: the drill bit hung in open air, and below it, flat and perfect as a mirror, was a sheet of what looked like brushed titanium, crisscrossed with veins of pale blue light, pulsing in perfect time with the frequency she’d logged earlier.

Carter let out a low whistle.

O’Neill, less impressed, said, “It looks like the inside of a high-end laptop. Only angrier.”

Daniel angled closer. “That’s not just a power conduit. It’s… it’s a circuit. Maybe a signal repeater.”

O’Neill: “Please tell me it doesn’t have Wi-Fi.”

Carter ignored the banter, snapped a still from the feed, and sent it up the chain to the science desk at SGC. She toggled her comm to the upper platform: “Teal’c, do you have visual?”

Teal’c’s reply came through with the calm precision of someone who’d never met a winter he couldn’t out-stare. “I do, Major Carter.”

“Thoughts?” she prompted.

There was a long silence, just the hiss of wind and the distant, rhythmic clank of the other rigs. Then: “This technology is not of the Goa’uld. Nor of the Ancients. It is… similar to the constructs found on P3X-984. But not identical.”

Carter’s memory supplied the reference: an outpost, long-dead, built by a species they still hadn’t catalogued.

Daniel wrote something in his notebook, then looked up. “Teal’c, can you read the inscription on the panel? There—lower right quadrant, just above the seam.”

Teal’c did not hesitate. “It is not a language I recognize. But the symmetry is deliberate.”

O’Neill said, “If you want symmetry, I can show you my old report cards. Straight C’s, all the way down.”

Carter keyed in the coordinates for the platform’s external sensor, sending the feed to Teal’c’s tablet. She watched as he panned in on the corner, the angular junction she’d noticed in the original data set. It was even more striking up close: a perfect, repeating motif, the angles too crisp to be anything but manufactured.

Teal’c’s voice, softer: “I have seen this motif before. On a device used for containment.”

Daniel scribbled furiously. “Containment of what?”

Teal’c: “Energy. Or perhaps something alive. The context was unclear.”

O’Neill, sotto voce: “I’m going to start voting ‘none of the above’ on all away missions, from here on out.”

Carter reran the frequency sweep. “If it’s a containment vessel, it’s still intact. The structure is stable. No signs of breach or decay.”

The tech at Rig Three—now openly sweating despite the cold—called up. “Should I retract the bit, or…?”

Carter hesitated, then: “Pause all mechanical movement. Hold at current depth.”

She sent the order to the other three rigs, locking them in place. The platform creaked in protest, a low groan that vibrated through the soles of her boots. For a moment, all was still except for the wind and the soft whir of the portable’s fan.

Daniel’s voice was uncharacteristically hushed. “If this is a containment structure, and we just drilled into it—”

Teal’c: “We should proceed with caution.”

O’Neill: “Carter, remind me again why the Air Force sends us instead of robots?”

Carter smiled, just a flicker, then said, “Because robots can’t improvise.”

She brought the camera back up, increased the resolution, and toggled through the spectrum. The blue veins pulsed, steady, now accompanied by a faint afterglow in the UV that she hadn’t noticed before. The energy signature was climbing, slow but undeniable.

Carter turned to O’Neill. “Sir, we need to inform command. And we need a protocol for first contact, in case whatever’s down there decides to say hello.”

O’Neill nodded, eyes still glued to the monitor. “Make the call, Carter. And maybe—” his gaze flicked to the feed, to the sharp corner and the logic of the pattern—“maybe tell them to read the manual before they poke it again.”

Carter grinned. “I’ll put it in the report.”

As she prepped the transmission, she caught Teal’c watching her, the angle of his head just slightly off—thoughtful, not worried, but not casual either. He nodded once, as if to say, It is your turn, Major Carter.

She keyed in the address for SGC, attached the priority tag, and in the body of the message wrote: “Contact with unknown structure. Possible containment. Recommend immediate escalation.”

She sent it.

As the wind battered the platform and the blue veins below flickered to a more insistent tempo, Carter wondered if maybe, just once, the surprise would be a good one.

But even she had to admit: the odds were not in their favor.

Daniel caught her glance and raised his mug, as if toasting the unknown. O’Neill rolled his eyes but didn’t disagree.

They stood together, three points on a frozen scaffold, staring at the future through a borrowed lens.

The veins pulsed, waiting for the world to catch up.

Chapter 28: The Sorcerer's Apprentice

Scene 1

Scene 1

The library’s after-hours hush was punctuated by the tick of Giles’s wind-up desk clock and the click of Ethan Rayne’s battered occult meter, whose needle vibrated in the red every time someone spoke above a certain threshold. Ethan had the thing laid out like it was a remote detonator for a science fair, brass case dented and inscribed with more than one set of initials. He rolled the dial with his thumb, squinted, and lined up the face against the edge of Giles’s blotter, as if triangulating the existential location of the meeting itself.

Willow sat cross-legged under the window, the screen of her laptop etching highlights into her face. She typed with both hands but her gaze flicked constantly between the display and Ethan’s movements, as if expecting him to sprout a second, more dangerous sensor and make off with the readings. Her hair was back in a ponytail, but two strands kept escaping to shadow her jaw. The legal pad next to her foot was half-filled with dates and packet numbers, each in a different color of gel pen.

Giles anchored the desk, three monographs splayed open and a yellow legal pad beside them, the left margin heavy with underlines. He kept shifting his gaze between the hand-drawn map Ethan had produced and the printed city plat Willow had summoned from the archive. Whenever Ethan made a mark on the map, Giles double-checked it against the plat, his pencil tapping a staccato on the edge of the desk. Every third or fourth tap, he’d stop, close his eyes, and rub at the bridge of his nose, glasses in hand. It was a negotiation: his old way of seeing the world versus the new math of it.

Ethan’s finger hovered over the map. “Here,” he said, voice calibrated for maximum clarity. “Packard Street. Cluster of five, possibly six, readings. Highest spike at the old cold storage warehouse, but the pattern radiates down the block.” He looked up at Giles. “This is deliberate. Not random.”

Giles squinted, then flipped a page in his notebook. “You’re certain it’s not demonic?”

Ethan’s lip curled. “Not even close. Demonic work is bombast. This is… institutional. Neat. Like someone doing lines of code, but in blood and ammonia.”

Willow, barely glancing up, said, “The monitoring array on the perimeter flagged a matching spike five hours before the last anchor break. And then again just before the incident at City Hall.” She turned the laptop so Ethan could see. “Look at the frequency hop. It’s almost perfectly in phase with your readings.”

He frowned, leaning in. “They’re running the same diagnostic as you. Maybe better.”

Giles absorbed that, mouth tight. “Which means they’ve been watching us longer than we thought.”

“Long enough to pattern-match our response times,” Willow said. “The working isn’t just a barrier. It’s an adaptive profile. Every time we hit it, it learns.”

Ethan cocked his head at Giles, almost smug. “Told you they’d go machine-learning before the Council did.”

Xander, leaning against the door with his hands in the pockets of a flannel overshirt, said, “So, wait. We’re being stalked by evil grad students with an algorithm?”

Buffy, perched on the radiator with her jacket zipped and boots already laced, tapped the toe of her boot on the floor. “What’s the move? We sit here and get studied, or we break their little science project?”

Willow ran her finger along the screen, then tapped a blinking line of text. “It’s not just collection. They’re feeding back into the local environment. The boundary work last night was tuned to repel only after a certain physical threshold. Someone’s modeling how hard we hit, and then reinforcing.”

Ethan closed the meter, snapping it shut with a click. “It’s too clean to be self-taught. There’s a framework here. I’d bet cash on a Wolfram and Hart instructional subroutine. They license these to hedge fund magicians and city managers in the know. Only this time, someone’s optimized for combat testing.”

Giles winced, the phrase leaving a visible mark. “So it’s a curriculum.”

“A beta test,” Ethan said, with a touch of respect.

Xander straightened, rolling his shoulder against the door frame. “So they’ve been doing homework on us.”

Willow nodded. “Final exam’s coming up, and I don’t think we’re the ones grading.”

Buffy stood, zipped her jacket the last half-inch, and looked at the address on the map. “Then let’s go see what they’ve learned.”

Ethan rolled up the map, tucked the sensor into his jacket, and grinned. “Always did love fieldwork.”

Giles closed the books in sequence, made a notation on the legal pad, and slid his glasses into place. He looked at Willow, then at Xander, and then at Buffy, as if cataloguing the present for insurance. “Be careful. The first move will be data gathering. The second will be more direct.”

Buffy smiled, teeth flat and even. “If they want direct, I can give them a tutorial.”

The four of them walked out, Ethan leading with the sensor half-concealed, Willow with her laptop clamped to her chest, Giles trailing with a hand in his pocket and the other clutching the legal pad. Xander closed the door behind them, then hesitated, looking at the silent clock on the wall.

He said, just loud enough for Giles to hear, “It’s always about the paperwork.”

Giles smiled without turning. “Welcome to the modern occult.”

The night outside was cold and bright, a sodium haze over the empty streets. Packard Street waited, and so did whatever was learning from them.

Buffy set the pace.

Scene 2

Scene 2

The trek to Packard Street was conducted at a brisk, mutual-dare pace. There were no cars. The street was flanked by dead lots and decomposing strip malls, each store window opaqued by layers of sun-bleached posters or tinfoil insulation. Two blocks in, the sodium lamp Willow had clocked from the main drag revealed itself to be a total liar: up close, its glow was patchy, rippling with the faint pulse of a busted ballast. Anything more than twenty feet away dissolved into a matte, uniform gray.

They cut through a lot full of decaying vending machines, then skirted a dented cyclone fence. Ethan stopped at the corner, sensor out, and gave a sharp “hold.” He flicked the meter on, and the needle leapt, hovered, then fell back by half, jittering as if the air were full of hidden fish. Willow, breathing fast but steady, opened her laptop and watched as the packet log started feeding her fresh, local noise.

Buffy took point, eyes on the warehouse, posture loose but predatory. Xander followed two paces behind, hands in the open, every line of his body broadcasting “not here for trouble, but ready to eat it if served.” Giles stayed well behind, tracking the perimeter in a slow, orbiting path, legal pad in hand and pencil ready, as if he could will the laws of magic to comply with Roberts Rules.

The warehouse itself was a one-story sheet-metal fossil, with its roll-up door secured by a chain so old it looked more likely to powder than to break. The side entrance was a punched-out fire door, barely hanging on its frame. There was a clean oval in the dust where the knob had been used recently, and a boot heel print, not even twelve hours old, pointed in. The ground outside was scuffed clean of debris in a three-foot path leading up to the entrance.

Ethan motioned everyone to keep tight and led the entry. He rolled his feet as if sneaking up on a sleeping bear. Inside, the air was bitter-cold and flavored with the metallic tang of ammonia. The warehouse had been gutted: no pallets, no crates, just an empty floor scored with forklift tire tracks and the marks of past, failed tenants. The only real feature was the network of old refrigeration tracks bolted to the ceiling, now home to the occasional seagull nest or industrial-sized cobweb.

But the walls—every wall—had been stenciled with sigils. Some were jagged, fresh, and black as printer toner; others had been overlaid in blue spray paint, and a third layer, darker and spattered, suggested something organic. The effect was less satanic than it was code: lines and circles, recursive logic, diagrams meant for another kind of mind.

Ethan’s meter beeped. “Peak is dead ahead, twenty meters.” He pointed at the center of the warehouse floor, where concentric rings of runic script had been drawn in what looked like a mixture of table salt and filings shaved from actual iron. The rings weren’t perfectly round, but close. The innermost circle contained a single figure, his back to the entrance, hunched forward in the classic pose of “working at something.”

Xander’s first instinct was “another Ethan” but the size was off, too slight and too fast—hands moving in short, jerky cuts, drawing on the air in front of him as if there was glass between him and the rest of the world.

Willow, whispering: “He’s running the script. In real time.”

Buffy: “How old is this one?”

Xander: “My age. Maybe younger.”

Ethan crouched, extended his arm to block the others, and mouthed, Don’t rush him.

But it was too late for stealth. The kid—he was a kid, Xander saw now, not even driving age—spun on his heel, both hands up, palms wide. The look on his face wasn’t rage; it was the pure, glassy shock of a deer right before the hood ornament. For an instant, the lines of blue-white light that had been arcing between his fingers snapped into a single, trembling vector, aimed dead at the group.

Xander said, “Wait, we’re—” but the kid’s hands jerked in a gesture neither practiced nor safe, and a bolt of fire the width of a flashlight blasted straight at Xander’s chest.

The impact was all heat and static, a punch of light followed by the smell of burning cotton. Xander rocked back, the top button of his flannel vaporizing, but the rest of the blast just rolled over his torso, dissipating in a haze of ozone. The kid gawked, visibly stunned that Xander hadn’t been incinerated.

Buffy advanced, silent and very fast, fanning out to the left flank. Ethan dropped low, muttering in a language Xander had never heard and probably didn’t want to. Willow set her laptop on the nearest concrete ledge and typed, one hand tracing the incoming data even as she tracked the fight.

The kid tried again, this time hurling two wild arcs in succession: one went high, clipping a refrigeration track and dumping rust and corroded dust down on the field, the other smashed into the far wall, scoring a line of melted cinder block and blowing a cloud of concrete dust out into the chain-link yard behind. The noise was astronomical, but the blast still wasn’t controlled. This was no seasoned villain. It was a kid running on desperate adrenaline and the ghost of a bad lesson.

Buffy zigged, drawing attention, while Xander walked forward, hands open and palms empty, voice as calm as he could muster. “Hey. You don’t have to—”

The kid backpedaled, smearing his heels through the inner circle, then flicked his left wrist in a weird staccato. There was a sharp, sour tang of burning metal, and suddenly a glyph on the ground at the far exit started to glow orange. A trap, Xander realized—a pressure glyph, probably designed to set the whole warehouse ablaze or collapse the salt circle.

But the kid’s hands were shaking so badly that he failed to lock the pattern, and the glyph fizzled, a weak strobe that faded back to gray. Xander closed the distance in three slow steps, got both hands on the kid’s wrists, and locked them in a grip that was gentle but utterly final. There was no snap, no crunch, no cry. Just resistance, then nothing, then a slow, wilting collapse.

The kid slumped, knees hitting the floor, and let out a thin, wet sound—not a wail, not a sob, just the spent exhale of a balloon that knows it’s not getting reinflated. Xander knelt, released one wrist, and kept the other in a loose hold just in case.

Buffy held at perimeter, watching for movement. Willow, seeing it was over, paused the script and exhaled. Ethan, crouched over the sigil at their feet, began reading it in a low, even voice, translating the structure aloud.

“Wolfram & Hart’s own grammar. They build these for compliance—feed in the energy, enforce the pattern. He didn’t write this. He was given it. The rest is just brute force.”

Xander loosened his grip, but didn’t let go entirely. “Hey,” he said, voice level. “Nobody’s going to hurt you. But you need to stop now. Okay?”

The kid looked up, face ghost-white, lips trembling. There was a raw burn along his left forearm, where the latest blast had scorched through the sleeve. Under the grime, the features were plain, familiar, a face that could have belonged to any kid at Sunnydale High.

Ethan, still reading, added: “They pushed him to burnout. That’s the point. These setups don’t expect the operator to survive.”

Buffy, inching closer, saw the way the kid’s hoodie hung off his shoulder, the frayed seam mended with dental floss. She holstered her stake. “He’s not the enemy.”

Willow said, “He’s a vector. A test case.”

The kid’s head dropped again. This time the tears came, but it wasn’t loud or performative—just the steady leak of someone who’d run out of defenses.

Xander shifted his grip, let the other wrist go, and waited.

After a long minute, the kid whispered, “Are you going to kill me?”

Xander shook his head. “Not tonight. Not ever.”

Buffy walked over, crouched so she was eye-to-eye. “What’s your name?”

The kid wiped his nose, not looking up. “Ben.”

Ethan said, “He doesn’t know who sent him, just where to be and what to do. They recruited him on a message board, probably. Promised him power, then dropped the real work on top of him.”

Willow said, “That’s how they scale. They’re not building armies; they’re running a lab.”

Giles, who had drifted closer during the aftermath, glanced at Ethan. “Is there a reversal, or a safe deactivation?”

Ethan frowned. “Not really. But if you short the sigil here—” he pointed at a break in the salt ring—“it will close the loop without a blast.”

Willow, quick, said: “If we don’t, does it go off on a timer?”

Ethan: “No timer. But if someone steps in the circle, or tries to leave, it’ll detonate.”

Buffy gave Xander a look. He got the message, then asked the kid, “Can you stand up?”

Ben tried, knees knocking, but managed it with Xander’s help. Buffy held out her hand, palm up. “May I?”

Ben hesitated, then nodded. She took his hand, led him out of the circle, and waited for Ethan to do his thing. Ethan grabbed a handful of salt from the perimeter, flicked it into the break in the line, and said a sharp word. There was a flash, not much brighter than a camera strobe, and the ring lost all charge.

Willow, already at her laptop, began cataloguing the sigil pattern, uploading the log in real time. “We need to get a copy to Carter. She’ll want to see what this looks like from the outside.”

Ben, swaying, said, “Can I go?”

Xander said, “Not yet. But soon. Do you need a doctor?”

Ben shook his head, but the shake was too small to be convincing.

Buffy said, “We’ll take you somewhere safe. After that, you can choose.”

Ben nodded, the relief visible in every muscle.

Giles made a note, tore the page from his pad, and handed it to Ethan. “Evidence for the record. Document everything. We’ll need it.”

Ethan accepted the page with a nod, then looked at Ben. “You have a future, if you want one. That’s more than most of the people who ran this playbook.”

Ben’s voice was thin, but audible. “I didn’t want to hurt anyone.”

Buffy, with the faintest of smiles, said, “That’s a good start.”

They walked Ben out together, Giles holding the door, Willow taking the rear with her laptop still running diagnostics. Ethan paused on the threshold, sensor in one hand, and glanced back at the wall of sigils, the paint already flaking at the edges. “They’re watching us,” he said, quiet.

Xander, almost cheerful, replied, “Let ‘em.”

The night outside was unchanged: cold, silent, with the flicker of the sodium lamp struggling to keep the dark at bay. But the world inside the group had moved a little. Maybe enough.

They walked Ben home.

Scene 3

Scene 3

Xander thumbed the signal on his wristwatch—three short, two long, just like Carter’s training. He’d never actually used it outside of drills. The first time, she’d made him test it by sending a message from under the metal stairs at school, just to see if it would bounce off the Army’s own bandwidth. It had. Now, it pulsed green at the edge, and a voice in his head started counting seconds from the moment he made contact.

Ten minutes and fifty-four seconds later, Chegwidden’s team rolled up in a black SUV so nondescript it made the nearby shopping carts look interesting by comparison. Two men in civilian parkas set up an impromptu perimeter, one at each end of the warehouse block; a third, wearing the medical patch, hoisted a hard case and double-timed it to the side entrance. Chegwidden himself came last, walking with the even, practiced stride of someone who’d spent half his life in places where you measured risk by the inch.

Inside the warehouse, Ben sat on the floor against the cinder block wall. The medic squatted in front of him, speaking quietly, the movements careful and nonthreatening. Ben’s legs were pulled up tight, but his face had gone from terror to pure exhaustion. He didn’t resist the blood pressure cuff or the pulse oximeter, didn’t even flinch when the alcohol swab touched his arm.

Chegwidden did a sweep of the sigil room, noting the scorch on the floor, the blown-out patch on the far wall, and Xander’s blackened shirt collar. He registered these things the way other people count the number of chairs at a meeting—inputs for a later summary, not a cause for drama. Then he walked over to Xander, took his measure, and said, “Threat neutralized?”

Xander didn’t hesitate. “He’s not a threat. He’s a product.”

Chegwidden’s face didn’t twitch, but his voice lost any hint of performative courtesy. “Standing protocol is neutralize and contain. Hostile practitioner in the field—same as a bomb with legs. You know the rationale.”

Xander looked at Ben, who was now allowing the medic to wrap a silver foil blanket around his shoulders. “You can have the rationale. But he’s not a biohazard. He’s a target because someone taught him to be.”

Chegwidden stepped two paces away, motioned for Xander to follow, and dropped his voice to the floor. “You are not wrong. But you are not entirely right.” He glanced over his shoulder. “You don’t get to rewrite the playbook every time you meet someone who looks like you used to.”

Xander felt the hit, but didn’t look away. “I’m not asking for special treatment. I’m telling you that if this goes down like the others, and you red-bag him, it’s going to go on a loop. For years.”

Chegwidden waited, the way a man might wait for a child to finish screaming before starting CPR. “We don’t want it on a loop,” he said. “That’s the whole reason we’re here.” He lifted his chin at Ben. “That one’s different. Is that what you’re saying?”

Ethan, who had spent the last five minutes pretending to photograph the wall sigils with his phone, chimed in without looking up. “He’s a weaponized vector, Admiral. Not a willful actor. The real question is whether he was supposed to go off, or just be seen.”

Chegwidden considered that, then turned to the medic and said, “Get him stabilized. Secure transport to Coronado, Code Three. No sedation unless medically required. Full chain of custody on the handoff.”

He didn’t rescind the rules. He just didn’t need to. The medic stood, gave Chegwidden a silent nod, and helped Ben up, hand under his elbow. Ben walked out under his own power, eyes on the ground, blanket pulled tight. The other two support staff had the site taped in orange cord by then, nothing left but the residue of weird and the stench of salt in the air.

Chegwidden waited until the van doors closed behind Ben, then turned back to Xander. “You do realize,” he said, “the other side wanted to see how you’d handle it.”

Xander shrugged. “Didn’t expect to be graded tonight.”

Ethan finally lowered the phone. “If it helps, you scored high marks for empathy. I’d call it a pass/fail, but these people don’t believe in binary outcomes.”

Chegwidden glanced at the wall, then at Ethan. “You get anything useful?”

Ethan scrolled his photo roll, showed the screen. “Working was Wolfram & Hart. Training-level stuff, but with high-voltage channeling. Someone wanted to know how much power a kid could pull before the system locked up or melted down. They ran a local; next time, maybe they’ll try remote.”

Chegwidden’s face did not move. “What do you recommend?”

Ethan gave a smile that was as thin as the air in the freezer. “Keep the incident report loose. Let them think you’re still following their script. Meanwhile, tighten the net on any new hires in the local area. And if you see another signature like this, consider running a counter-campaign. Make them flinch first.”

Chegwidden nodded. “Noted.”

The three of them stood there, triangle of stillness, for an awkward second that felt longer than it was. Then Chegwidden said, “You’ll want to get that burn checked out, Mr. Harris.”

Xander looked down at the scorched shirt, flexed his shoulder, and said, “I’ll live.”

Chegwidden almost smiled. “That’s the plan. Walk me out?”

They did, Chegwidden in front, Xander and Ethan behind, the two support staff already pulling down the cordon and logging the last details on a legal pad. Outside, the cold was sharper, the wind blowing in from the dead lots with an edge that sliced through every layer.

At the car, Chegwidden said, “We’ll follow up in forty-eight hours. If there are repercussions from tonight, you’ll get a direct call.” He looked at Ethan. “You, I assume, will be monitoring.”

Ethan nodded, slid his phone away, and said, “I always do.”

Chegwidden got in the car, closed the door, and left the window down just long enough to say, “Thank you for your discretion.”

The SUV pulled away, the sodium light briefly painting the side in pale fire before it turned the corner and vanished.

Ethan stood beside Xander for a moment, watching the empty street. “You think they’re done?” he asked.

Xander shook his head. “No one ever is.”

Ethan nodded, satisfied. “That’s why you get to keep trying.”

They walked back toward Main, hands in pockets, not saying much, but matching strides. The night was quiet, the evidence all bagged and logged, but the feeling in the air was different: something had learned, and something else had survived. For the moment, that would have to be enough.

At the crosswalk, Xander stopped, looked at the bandage on his arm, and said, “Thanks for the backup, Ethan.”

Ethan grinned. “Thank yourself, kid. I just read the lines.”

They crossed the street, two shadows in the long, orange light, headed for the future one case at a time.

Chapter 29: Tech Transfer

Scene 1

The cargo bay wasn’t designed for humans, let alone four SGC engineers, two scientists, and enough salvaged technology to outfit a regional diagnostic lab. Every time the door cycled, a new sheet of Antarctic air curled in, fogging up the first meter of space and coaxing visible breath from anyone not lucky enough to be fully covered in thermal layers. The lighting rig overhead was SGC surplus, twenty halogens bolted to a lattice of Starfleet conduit, and it pulsed at a frequency that made time feel slower, or at least flatter.

Jenny Calendar had Beverly Crusher’s hands. She never thought about it in those words, but the way her fingers aligned a circuit, the way the calibration tool hovered above the contact, that came from thirty years of experience that were never supposed to be hers. Across the workbench, Carter watched the feed on a rig-mounted monitor, her eyes flicking between the numbers and the live trace, not quite smiling but fully present.

The sensor core was a stripped Goa’uld array, lifted from a ribbon device they’d neutralized last month. Carter had dissected it with a scalpel and a jeweler’s loupe, then rebuilt it in a form factor slightly larger than a paperback—thick, dense, the kind of thing you could drop from a roof and expect to work. The display was a repurposed Starfleet data padd, but its interface had been rewritten from scratch; the sweep line glowed a jittery green, stuttering every time Jenny tweaked the micro-tuner at the base.

The SGC engineers crowded close, four of them, their jackets still dusted with snow from the walk across the bay. One of them, a bearded guy with the name tape STANTON, took notes with an actual pencil on a clipboard the color of dried mustard. The rest watched in a way that made Jenny think of grad students at a department seminar: eager, ready to pounce on a misstep but also hoping to witness something they could someday claim as their own.

Carter said, “Ready on three,” and Jenny nodded, fitting the final coupling into the slot with a satisfying metallic click. The sweep line on the padd stilled, hovered, then jumped from green to a low, steady amber.

The room went quiet except for the soft rush of the air circulator.

“That’s a target lock,” Carter said. She said it with the calm of someone who’d just observed a chemical reaction she predicted but never quite believed would happen in the wild.

Jenny set the tool down, stretched her fingers, and made herself look at the display before she looked at Carter. “What’s the accuracy?”

Carter pressed a sequence on the side panel. “Six milliradians, plus or minus two, at this temperature. Better than the ribbon’s original.” She looked over at the engineers. “Mark it—full success on the static test.”

Stanton made a note on the clipboard. The others leaned in, squinting at the readout, maybe hoping to catch a glitch or an unexpected jump in the trace.

Jenny flexed her left hand, shook off the pins-and-needles that still showed up when she worked too long without stopping. She reached for the power bypass, disengaged it, then began walking the engineers through the reversal sequence. “You go cold before the power cell swap. If you don’t, you risk a resonance spike—see here, the insulation’s not built for continuous load. Pull the secondary, then remove the core. If you get a red bar, restart from zero and let the system drain for fifteen seconds.”

Carter repeated the steps, then had one of the engineers try it, hands shaking just a bit as he handled the module. When he got the sequence right, Jenny said, “Good,” and didn’t bother hiding the small note of pride. She turned to Carter. “Want to show them the frequency calibration?”

Carter picked up the padd, toggled the diagnostic menu, and guided them through the process. “For a naquadah-specific resonance, you cycle the sweeps from twenty-two hertz to fifty. If you go higher, you start to pick up interference from the ship’s hull. We’ll have to isolate the spectrum when we get this outside, but for now—” she gestured at the sweep line, “—the pattern is distinct enough to run a real-world calibration without pulling too much power.”

One of the engineers—long hair, nose chapped from the cold—asked, “What about nulls? The last test, the signal just dropped off the board.”

Jenny said, “You get a null if the field is shielded by something more dense than what the probe can handle. Or if the EM background here—” she tapped the bulkhead, “—is bleeding too much. The Hellmouth’s resonance can overload a standard SGC probe in about four minutes. This model runs a three-step reboot: full power down, cold restart, and a secondary sweep with a lower integration time.”

Carter watched the faces as she said it, then added, “If you’re running field tests in Sunnydale, or anywhere in a three-mile radius, expect to lose the signal every so often. That’s not a failure. It’s the environment pushing back.”

The engineers copied that word for word. Jenny could see their hands moving in near-perfect unison, the ritual of transcribing something from a mentor whose respect they wanted to earn.

They spent another half hour running the procedure, each engineer cycling through the steps: power down, battery swap, cold restart, recalibrate. Stanton logged each round, his notes getting tighter and more organized as the sequence became muscle memory. Jenny watched, corrected only when she needed to, and let herself enjoy the feeling of actually building a thing with people who cared if it worked.

When the fourth test was finished, Carter motioned everyone back. “You’ve got the protocol,” she said. “Now we write the checklist. Nobody runs a field test without this signed off by both labs.”

She pulled a legal pad from her vest pocket and began drafting the steps in her block handwriting. Jenny dictated from memory, adjusting Carter’s phrasing where the SGC protocol diverged from what Starfleet would expect. “We need to add a caution about the zeroing—if the core gets stuck, you run the risk of a brownout.”

Carter nodded, penciled in the caveat, and then made a bold heading: FIELD DEPLOYMENT CHECKLIST. She wrote each step as a bullet point, then handed it to Stanton. “Make three copies. Everyone signs.”

Jenny watched as the engineers took turns with the pad, their gloves coming off for the signature, each one treating the act like the final step in a ritual.

There was no fanfare at the end. Carter and Jenny took the signed sheets, then walked to the side alcove where the SGC team lead waited with a clipboard of his own. The man—gray hair, windburned cheeks, reading glasses perched on the end of his nose—held up the one-page memorandum of understanding.

“Are you ready?” he asked.

Jenny said, “Let’s do it.”

They signed, Carter first, then Jenny, the pens making tiny indentations in the paper as the ink soaked in. The team lead witnessed, signed, then set the page in a folder labeled JOINT TECHNICAL SHARING.

Carter shook Jenny’s hand, once, firm and professional, and the SGC team lead gave a brisk “Thank you, Doctor, Major,” before heading off to the next protocol in the queue.

Jenny looked at the prototype on the bench. She picked it up, turned it over in her hands, and set it back down, just so.

The amber light from the display was the only thing that felt even a little bit warm in the bay.

Scene 2

There was a distinct flavor to Situation Room mornings, even at nine p.m. The upholstery never aired out, so the room always smelled a little like suit lining and nervous breath. The coffee—if you could call it that—had the personality of burnt hair, and there was a faint, undercurrent tang of ozone from the projector that hadn’t been serviced since the Clinton years.

President Bartlet sat at the head of the table. His jacket hung on the chairback in that deliberate way that said “informal, but only just.” His tie was loosened but not sloppy. The binder in front of him was still open to the classified summary sheet, but he hadn’t touched it in at least ten minutes. Instead, he rotated a capped pen in his right hand, over and under, over and under, never once dropping it.

The Joint Chiefs lined both sides of the table, each with their own binder and their own brand of fatigue. General Akers, Air Force, was halfway through a legal pad of scribbled notes and on his third coffee. Admiral Fitzwallace, Navy, had his hands folded on the table, unmoving. General Staub, Army, glared at the cover of his binder as if it were personally responsible for every procurement boondoggle in the last decade.

Weir was on the screen, muted for the time being, a half-page of comments already flagged for her next input window.

The briefer, a civilian from the National Security staff, did not waste time on the metaphors. “In summary: the sensor suite’s detection range is approximately five kilometers, depending on mass. The minimum threshold for naquadah resonance is four hundred grams, with a margin for error at high-EM sites. The sweeps are externally powered—no internal battery to spoof—and the signature profile is distinct from natural uranium or plutonium. The core is a Goa’uld artifact, but the assembly is all-American. The attached annex covers field deployment and maintenance.”

He paused, let the silence find its way into the corners of the room, then said, “The debate is on how to share the technology.”

General Akers leaned in. “Our SIGINT partners need this as soon as we can get it to them. If the Chinese get wind of a deployment and we haven’t already seeded the Five Eyes, we’re inviting a global incident.”

Staub cut in: “We classify it at the highest level, issue it only to Tier One response teams, and keep the specs in-house until we have a countermeasure.”

Admiral Fitzwallace’s voice was the gravel and smoke of old command: “If we move too slow, we get caught with our pants down. If we move too fast, every adversary on the planet knows we’ve got it. Either way, there’s no putting the toothpaste back in the tube.”

Bartlet twirled the pen again. “What about the Starfleet origin? If someone takes one apart and sees what’s in the core?”

The briefer said, “We’re repackaging all exterior casings with SGC hardware. The internal signature can only be detected by specialized tools—and those don’t exist outside of SGC and Starfleet. As long as no one does a full reverse-engineer—”

General Akers: “Someone always does a reverse-engineer.”

Bartlet set the pen down, flat on the table. “If we send these into the field, and something happens, and a partner government runs with the detection but doesn’t know the source, and then people die—whose call is that?”

The silence was longer this time.

Staub: “We can’t share what we don’t understand, sir. Not without risk.”

Bartlet tapped the binder, twice. “So the debate is between sharing a black box and risking the consequences, or not sharing and accepting the risk of being caught off guard.”

Fitzwallace nodded. “That’s the trade, Mr. President.”

Bartlet reached for the coffee, grimaced, and set it aside without drinking. “Here’s what we’ll do. You share the detection data with partner agencies, but on a need-to-know, case-by-case basis. If it’s operationally necessary, you use existing liaison channels. But the specs—the tech, the Starfleet provenance—those stay in this building and nowhere else, unless and until I say otherwise. You have forty-eight hours to get me a draft protocol.”

He let that land, then closed the binder, slow and final.

“Any questions?”

Nobody had any. He looked around the table, and for a moment, the only sound was the projector fan, laboring against the heat of a world that did not want to slow down.

“Good,” said Bartlet. “Let’s get it done.”

The Chiefs filed out in silence, each with their own reading of what had just been decided. Bartlet stayed behind, eyes on the muted Weir, who now sat on her end of the video link in a room even less forgiving than the Sit Room.

From the doorway, Leo McGarry gave a quick, tight nod: the silent heads-up that the MOU was signed, that Carter’s team had the prototype, that the next phase was already in motion.

Bartlet met the look, nodded back, and let out a breath.

There was always a next phase.

Scene 3

It was the kind of dusk that made the whole school look like a set for a TV pilot: the old gym’s exterior wall lit in pink and blue from the reflected sky, the parking lot empty except for a battered field van with the SGC logo stenciled discreetly near the rear bumper. The basketball court was a patchwork of cracked concrete, the paint lines so faded they might have been from another era entirely. Off in the distance, a sprinkler kicked on, its stutter and hiss just audible above the scrape of sneakers on the court.

Buffy leaned against the wall, arms folded, watching the two-person tech team wheel the sensor array out from the van. The device was everything Jenny had promised and Carter had iterated: boxy, rugged, no concessions to aesthetics except the green sweep line that ran across the data padd face in a smooth arc, back and forth, like a heartbeat at rest.

Xander, for his part, stood close enough to see the display but far enough to give the SGC operator his perimeter. He looked like a civilian observer in a war movie—aware, attentive, but not pretending to understand the script. The tech team wore SGC parkas over school-district-issue polos, which somehow made them look less official and more determined. The taller of the two, Santos, ran a standard grid pattern with the unit, calling out coordinates every four meters and logging them with a clicker that looked like it had been borrowed from a wildlife survey.

The sweep line glowed a steady green as Santos walked the length of the court. It cleared the parking lot, cleared the gym, then started a new pass aimed at the strip of grass between the gym and the library annex.

Buffy tracked the sensor with her eyes, almost bored, until the operator’s hand froze mid-step.

The sweep line stuttered, then went flat—a hard, cold red. The operator stared at the padd, eyebrows knitted, then tapped the power cycle. The line reset, tried a sweep, and flatlined again.

Xander asked, “Is it broken, or is that…?”

The operator hit the comm on her shoulder. “Carter, do you see this?”

On the hood of the van, a ruggedized laptop ran a video link straight to Cheyenne Mountain. Carter’s face, more gray in the image than usual, appeared above a scrolling bar of live data. Her voice, clean and tight through the laptop speaker, said, “Go to the secondary mode. Run the spectrum at quarter speed.”

The operator did as instructed. The sweep line crawled, so slow that Xander could see every micro-jump, every attempt the unit made to resync to baseline. This time, instead of red, the sweep paused over a three-meter section of lawn, oscillated between amber and crimson, then held steady at a blinking orange.

Buffy’s hand was already at her belt, stake out and gripped, before anyone spoke.

Carter’s voice: “That’s not a ghost reading. That’s a containment signature.” She rattled off three numbers, her tone going flatter by the second. “It’s below the detection threshold for the Mark I, but the new model is pulling the phase shift. Santos, can you mark the coordinates?”

Santos, who had not blinked since the first red sweep, said, “Marked. Should I go closer?”

Carter said, “No. Pull back. Export the log to the external drive, then run a compare against the Antarctic baseline, side-by-side.”

The operator did so, and within seconds, both screens—on the padd and on the laptop—showed a pair of jagged signatures. The Antarctic one was as expected: sharp, regular, like a hospital EKG. The Sunnydale trace was warped, the whole left side of the spike stretched, bent, almost as if something heavy had been sitting on it.

Buffy, eyes still locked on the grass, said, “Is it a bomb?”

Xander, not taking his eyes off the display: “Or is it alive?”

Carter, from the van: “We don’t know. The signature is masked. The EM bleed from the Hellmouth overlays everything, but this is the first time we’ve seen a secondary profile under the distortion.”

Santos asked, “Did we miss this in the first sweep?”

Carter: “It wasn’t visible before. The Hellmouth’s phase interference must have shifted, maybe after the last event.” There was a pause, the sound of Carter double-checking her own numbers. “Whatever it is, it’s been there a while.”

Buffy stepped forward, the motion almost lazy but perfectly controlled. “Show me where.”

Santos pointed, not with a gesture but with the whole device, holding it like a divining rod. The sweep line trembled as it neared the marked coordinates, then flared orange again.

Xander said, “That’s right under the north fence. Where the ground dips.” He turned to Buffy. “Remember when you said that part of the lawn felt off?”

She nodded. “Not in a Hellmouth way, either. Just… off.”

The operator held the device steady, then started a slow, circular motion. The orange spike grew sharper, a small volcano erupting in the middle of a calm sea.

On the van’s hood, the laptop beeped—a warning, not loud but sharp enough to make everyone turn. The second operator, a guy in glasses with the patience of a monk, ran a quick script and said, “There’s a cross-signal. The mask isn’t just hiding the signature, it’s redirecting it. Every time we run a sweep, the return shifts, like it’s tracking us back.”

Buffy didn’t move. She said, “Is it dangerous?”

Carter’s voice: “We don’t know yet. But don’t go closer. I’m pinging the Sunnydale site for a support team, just in case.”

Buffy’s eyes narrowed, but she stepped back. “If something comes out, I handle it.”

Xander looked at the numbers again. “Can we tell how deep it is?”

The operator checked, did a quick calculation, and said, “Less than two meters. Maybe a meter fifty, hard to say with the masking.” She looked at Xander. “You want to dig it up?”

He shook his head. “Not my call. But if it’s been here since before the new field sweeps, it’s probably not an accident.”

On the laptop, Carter and Jenny were now side by side in the video window, both watching the Sunnydale data stream in real time. Jenny leaned in, her face unreadable, and said, “Try a harmonic sweep at 17 hertz. It’s a resonance point for naquadah, but also for certain organic substrates.”

The operator nodded, set the sweep, and waited.

The sweep line slowed, then paused. The orange became yellow, then green, then—at the end of the sweep—jumped to a blinding white spike that saturated the display.

Everyone froze. The only sound was the distant sprinkler, ticking over the last arc of its cycle.

Carter, quietly: “That’s new.”

Buffy looked at the lawn, then at the sensor, and then at Xander. “What does it mean?”

Xander shook his head. “If it’s a trap, it’s a good one.”

Jenny’s voice, faint but clear: “It’s a communication. Or a warning.”

Carter said, “Get the unit back to the van. Upload the data to me, full packet. We need to run a comparative analysis before anyone touches the site.”

Buffy watched as the operators packed up, then holstered her stake. She didn’t put it away—just let it rest against her leg.

Xander said, “You want to call Willow?”

Buffy nodded. “Tell her we need every archive on signal masking, demon and otherwise.”

They walked back to the van together, the light from the gym wall fading as the sun dipped below the horizon.

On the hood, the laptop flickered. Carter and Jenny stared at the anomaly, their faces mirrored in miniature on the screen.

The sprinkler on the far lawn clicked off. The whole school fell silent.

For a moment, nobody moved. The only thing that seemed alive was the white spike on the padd, burning a hole through the quiet.

Xander looked at it, then at the sky, and wondered what you called a ghost when it was still alive and waiting.

Buffy watched the grass, her face unreadable.

On the laptop, Carter and Jenny exchanged a look. Neither smiled.

A breeze moved across the court, cold and sharp, and the world started up again.

✳

Carter and Jenny inventoried what the Enterprise could contribute if the invasion reached orbit: sensors, shield harmonics, power routing, and relay geometry. Every line carried a second column for what could be used without compromising the ship or violating Calendar’s authority.

“We can make the ship an early-warning platform,” Carter said. “Possibly a relay. Not a weapon unless we have no other choice.”

The defense was becoming real in layers: information, sensors, logistics, and only then force.

✳

Chapter 30: The Guardian

Scene 1

The drill went through on the first pass, but not with the cheap thrill of a breaking bottle—more like a failed tooth extraction. The corer ground through four meters of ice, then stuttered and screamed as the bit found the seam of non-terrestrial alloy: a matte, black curve, too geometric for geology, too precise for anything that ever froze on this continent by accident. At -48 Celsius, nothing was supposed to smell, but the moment the breach flexed, a gout of metallic air shot outward and snapped the first two rows of floodlights with an audible pop.

O’Neill, first in the shaft, muttered, “That’s new,” but nobody had time to process it. Carter was already pressing forward, shoulder to shoulder with Teal’c, and Daniel was close enough behind that the ice fog from their breath mixed, white and gray. Jenny Calendar, not technically on the team but seconded by the President himself, tracked the sensor display over Carter’s arm, her own hands clenching and unclenching, never steady for more than half a second.

Carter’s primary sense was the pain behind her left ear—the way any proper stress migraine started—but today the world arrived in slices: the whiplash cold, the ozone tingle of the powered glyphs coming online, the wash of Data’s diagnostic echo as it rippled through her jury-rigged array and told her, in no uncertain terms, that this was the right place.

The shaft widened as they entered the vault. The wall was a patchwork of repeating triangles, each the size of a coffin lid, each edge inlaid with a strand of something that looked like fiber optic but glowed at the interface with a low, deliberate blue. The first row of glyphs to Carter’s left flickered twice, then steadied, their logic already resolving into a syntax that made her teeth ache—digital, yes, but written in a grammar that reminded her of nothing so much as Daniel’s index cards.

Daniel, for his part, had one of those index cards already out, along with a stub pencil and a flashlight that he didn’t bother pointing anywhere but at the wall. He started muttering, “Repeating pattern… not Sumerian… oh, that’s interesting,” and Carter wanted to hit him for it, but she let him be.

The breach wasn’t a doorway so much as an invitation to claustrophobia. The space just inside was big enough for two—maybe three—people to stand abreast, but every line of sight ended in mirrored dark. The floor was not a floor at all, just more of the matte alloy, less slick than the outside but with a thermal that sucked the heat right through the boots.

O’Neill, still at point, swept the P90’s taclight in a slow arc. “If anyone’s home, they know we’re here,” he said, tone pitched for ‘muttering to the room’ rather than ‘giving orders.’

Teal’c stepped in, back to the side, weapon cradled not so much at ready as in a kind of coiled patience. He moved with the discipline of a man for whom nothing about this was especially weird, just a Tuesday with a new dialect.

The pressure differential settled after a minute. With the floodlights dead, the vault’s own logic took over: every wall segment lit up in sequence, like a child’s toy or a runway for something that did not want to fly. Jenny, eyes glued to the diagnostic, said, “There’s a heartbeat. The sequence is pacing to Data’s output.” Her breath hung in the cold, but her voice was clean and flat, no vibrato.

Carter’s wristwatch ticked against her glove, a single short haptic pulse. The one-way link to Xander. She let the vibration ground her, then bent to check the array’s output: the vault was not just echoing the surface signal, it was mimicking it, shaping its own reply in a slightly distorted but unmistakably familiar pattern.

The team moved forward, careful as surgeons. Teal’c led with his own intuition—“This way,” he said, and was always right by two meters. O’Neill kept a hand to the wall, the other on the weapon, and Daniel scribbled as he walked, never once tripping over his own boots. Carter tracked every voltage spike, every thermal micro-shift, logging as fast as the array could buffer. Jenny drifted behind her, a ghostly second shadow.

At the third corridor bend, the vault opened up. They could see the breach itself, still steaming in the -48C air, rimmed in a ring of glacial frost that had been flash-melted and then instantly re-frozen. The wall opposite the breach was concave, a parabola with its focal point about six meters back into the structure. The sense of being watched was immediate and total—Carter could feel her skin’s electric field prickling, as if every cell was shouting for her to run.

It was Daniel who stopped first, voice barely above a whisper. “Something’s—”

The rest was lost as the guardian stepped into the corridor from nowhere at all.

There was no flicker, no cinematic fade-in. The figure was simply there: a column of blue-white logic, roughly humanoid, but scaled up—at least 2.5 meters from heel to helmet. Its ‘face’ was a cluster of overlapping geometric planes, and where its eyes should be there was a void, a cutout against the brightness that seemed to bend the light into itself.

The wings unfolded with a subsonic hum, the kind of noise that made bones vibrate but left the ears wanting. Two arcs, one from each shoulder, swept out and back, then settled at a forty-five degree angle behind the guardian’s frame. The wings were not solid, but a lattice of photonic grid and dark between—each segment micro-diffracting the vault’s light into a halo that danced across the wall.

For a second, nobody moved.

Carter’s sensor array registered a spike: positronic signature, nearly Starfleet grade, but with a delta she couldn’t explain. Her head spun with the analogies—“Like Data, but not Data. Like his language was taught to a different species and then spoken back with a new accent.”

The guardian’s voice arrived not from its mouth but from the air itself, as if the chamber had learned to speak. Each word was deliberate, no wasted bandwidth.

“This repository is sealed under the authority of those who came before. State your designation and the nature of your claim.”

O’Neill’s weapon did not move, but his stance shifted—alert, but no longer expecting a firefight. He said, “Jack O’Neill. United States Air Force. Our claim is that we dug two miles down, so—” He trailed off, because the guardian did not acknowledge the humor.

Instead, the guardian’s targeting reticle—visible, a soft blue line that bisected each member’s chest for exactly one heartbeat—paused on Carter’s sensor. Then on Jenny. Then again on Carter. Each time, the wing arcs behind it flexed, the resonance in the air tightening.

Carter risked a glance at Jenny. “It’s not just watching,” she said, voice low. “It’s reading the sensor logic. And maybe us.”

Jenny’s lips pressed flat. “If it’s reading, it can hear us.”

Daniel, unable to help himself, stepped forward, index card in hand. “The wall glyphs,” he said, “they’re in sequence. They’re not a warning, they’re a process log. It’s not telling us to stay out. It’s outlining a protocol for entry.”

Teal’c, who had not flinched since the guardian appeared, adjusted his grip and said, “This architecture is unfamiliar. But I have seen similar containment constructs among the oldest worlds of the System Lords. They are always traps, but sometimes also doors.”

O’Neill gave a tiny nod. “Then let’s not knock the wrong way.”

The air between them and the guardian shimmered, and for an instant Carter thought it was about to attack. Instead, it spoke again. “Designation registered. Protocol requires demonstration of comprehension. Advance, but do not breach the secondary interface. Failure to comply will be met with resistance calibrated to match origin.”

Daniel’s eyes widened, as if he’d just been handed the answer sheet to an exam he was born to fail. “It’s going to test us.”

O’Neill’s shoulders relaxed, a millimeter, but he said, “We’re very good at tests.”

Carter pulled up the diagnostic feed, watching as the wall glyphs shifted to a new pattern—faster, the pulses now in perfect sync with Data’s onboard sequence. She wondered if the android on Deck Seven was awake, if his logic core was listening.

She glanced at Jenny. The woman was staring at the guardian, eyes dark, face set. Her voice was low but certain. “It will want more than an ID.”

O’Neill said, “Let’s not give it a reason to ask for a retina scan.”

They stepped forward as a unit, the guardian’s gaze never leaving Carter’s sensor. At three meters, it stopped them with a lift of one wing. “Secondary interface engaged.”

The wall at the end of the parabola slid open. Not a door, but a reconfiguration—panels shifting until there was a hexagonal aperture, black inside, with a lattice of blue like an afterimage on the retina. The air temperature dropped, if possible, another ten degrees. Breath fogged instantly, the white plume sucked inward as if the vault was inhaling.

Daniel, clutching his notebook, said, “It’s a lock and a key. The protocol is the key.”

Jenny said, “Or the protocol is the lock. If you don’t get it right…”

Teal’c: “Then the door remains closed. Or we do not survive the attempt.”

Carter, parsing the diagnostic, saw a new readout: the positronic signature on the guardian had started to echo back her own sensor logic. It was not just reading. It was learning.

She keyed the array, toggling to broadcast. “Starfleet diagnostic output, continuous.” She was aware this might do nothing, or everything.

The guardian’s wings flexed. The blue reticle flickered to a double helix, then faded.

“Protocol accepted,” said the vault. “Proceed.”

O’Neill looked at Carter. “You’re up.”

Carter stepped forward, past the cold, into the hexagon, every cell in her body screaming retreat, but her mind unable to resist the symmetry, the logic, the knowledge waiting on the other side.

Jenny went after, and Daniel and Teal’c behind. O’Neill brought up the rear, P90 raised, eyes never quite leaving the guardian until they were through.

The vault’s inner chamber was a shock of light and structure, shelves of crystal like the nervous system of a planet, blue veins pulsing in time with the heartbeat of the thing on Deck Seven and the mind of the woman who carried his knowledge.

Behind them, the guardian folded its wings and waited.

Carter did not know if they had just succeeded, or if they had become the experiment.

It didn’t matter. She advanced, array raised, ready to see what knowledge looked like at the end of the universe.

Scene 2

The standoff stretched. The air inside the vault was so cold Carter’s eyelashes started to stick, but the sweat on the back of her neck trickled anyway, prickling under the layers of synth-fleece and SGC-issue Nomex. O’Neill’s boots squeaked on the crystalline floor, a tiny, disrespectful noise in the chamber’s logic-precise silence.

“Jack O’Neill,” he said, breaking protocol by introducing himself to a weaponized hologram with wings. “United States Air Force. And our claim is that we drilled through about two miles of ice to get here, so.” He let the sentence finish itself with a minimalist gesture, as if handing off the next move to the room.

The guardian didn’t so much as blink. Its targeting reticle moved again, slow as a heartbeat, parsing each member of the group in sequence. It took two passes—O’Neill, Daniel, Teal’c, Carter, and then, oddly, Jenny. It hesitated on Carter’s sensor array, as if recognizing the signature, then locked onto Jenny’s face with a prolonged pause that was not quite human, but close enough for Carter to read it as an algorithmic stutter.

Daniel, undeterred by the lack of small talk, had already started whispering to himself, notebook open and pencil ready. The glyphs lining the inner wall flashed in a slow, deliberate sequence, and he echoed the pulse as he read them: “Causal… test. Purity-of-intent. Hierarchy of claim… Oh, that’s not good,” he said, louder than he meant to.

The wings of the guardian twitched, tightening their arcs. The voice came again, just as flat as before: “You have partial comprehension of the notation. Partial comprehension is insufficient for access authorization.”

Daniel, to his credit, did not freeze. He said, “I can translate more if you let me interact with the panel,” his tone part plea, part challenge.

The guardian ignored the request, or filed it away for later. Carter, watching the array, saw the positronic signature spike again. She said, “The diagnostic pulse your system is tracking originates from a positronic matrix aboard the surface vessel. Lieutenant Commander Data, USS Enterprise.” She let that hang for a beat, then added, “He’s damaged. He can’t be here in person.”

The silence that followed was not random. Three full seconds—enough for Carter to check the array, confirm the pulse, and register the temperature drop as the vault’s systems spun up in response.

Then: “The signal is confirmed. The matrix is incomplete. Incompleteness does not invalidate origin.”

Jenny’s jaw set, and she took a half step forward, boots crunching on the ice. “He is not incomplete,” she said. “He is injured. There is a difference, and if your system is sophisticated enough to read his diagnostic pulses, it is sophisticated enough to understand that distinction.”

The guardian’s head swiveled, just a hair, to face Jenny. The reticle flickered, then split into two, overlaying her face and her chest, reading not just her surface but her interior. Carter wondered what it saw: the tangle of two lives, the bleed of memory and experience. Jenny’s hands were fists now, but her voice held steady.

Teal’c, still at Carter’s side, finally spoke. “I have encountered the architecture of many civilizations. This construction does not belong to the Goa’uld. It predates their reach.”

The guardian’s gaze slid from Jenny to Teal’c, the blue light pulsing softer now. “Observation is accurate. This repository is legacy. It is not intended for immediate use by transient species.”

O’Neill, weapon still trained, but voice a shade lighter, said, “Then who is it for?”

The guardian replied, “Those who pass the protocol.”

Daniel, who had been reading faster, now said, “The panel reads like a—” He broke off, then found the phrase. “It’s a test of logic and cooperation. It wants a demonstration.”

O’Neill: “A science fair?”

“No,” said Carter. “More like a Turing test. For civilization.”

Daniel said, “That’s… exactly right. It wants to see if we work together. Not just here, but as a society.”

The guardian’s wings flexed, and the vault’s lighting shifted, glyphs running faster along the walls. The targeting reticle on Carter’s sensor array vanished. The voice returned: “Partial comprehension is augmented by intent. Proceed to interface and state your collaborative purpose.”

Jenny exhaled, a long cloud of breath. “It wants us to admit why we’re here.”

O’Neill: “That’s easy. We’re here because we want to know what’s in the vault.”

Carter shook her head. “Not enough. It wants more than curiosity. It wants intent.”

Daniel: “If I’m reading this right, the protocol wants a unified statement. Something that represents all of us, not just the loudest voice.”

Teal’c nodded. “A test of will, not of strength.”
